Notions of amalgamation for AECs and categoricity
Hanif Joey Cheung

Abstract
. Motivated by the free products of groups, the direct sums of modules, and Shelah’s ( λ , 2 ) (\lambda,2) -goodness, we study strong amalgamation properties in Abstract Elementary Classes. Such a notion of amalgamation consists of a selection of certain amalgams for every triple M 0 ≤ M 1 , M 2 M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2} , and we show that if K K designates a unique strong amalgam to every triple M 0 ≤ M 1 , M 2 M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2} , then K K satisfies categoricity transfer at cardinals ≥ θ ⁡ ( K ) + 2 LS ​ ( K ) \geq\theta(K)+2^{\text{LS}(K)} , where θ ⁡ ( K ) \theta(K) is a cardinal associated with the notion of amalgamation. We also show that if such a unique choice does not exist, then there is some model M ∈ K M\in K having 2 | M | 2^{|M|} many extensions which cannot be embedded in each other over M M . Thus, for AECs which admit a notion of amalgamation, the property of having unique amalgams is a dichotomy property in the sense of Shelah’s classification theory.



In
[
GL00
]
, Grossberg and Lessman derived a forking-like independence relation on an arbitrary pregeometry
(X,\text{cl})
, and showed that many of the defining properties of forking in a stable theory are also satisfied in this setting. A natural question to ask then is how to define such a relation on an AEC
(K,\leq)
where each model has a natural pregeometry which is also coherent with
K
(see section 7 for a formal definition of these notions), and what properties such a relation would satisfy (in comparison to, for example, a stable independence relation on some monster model of
K
). In particular, the property of types having a unique nonforking extension holds if there is a unique (up to isomorphism) amalgam of models
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
where the image of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are independent over
M_{0}
(with respect to the pregeometry of the amalgam). This suggests that for such a class, the structure of the class depends not only on assuming the amalgamation property, but how “well-behaved” the collection of such independent amalgams are. This is reminiscent of the “stable amalgams” first introduced by Shelah in
[
She83
]
and
[
She83a
]
, where the fact that stable amalgams can be extended and are preserved under continuous chains is used to construct a model in a higher cardinality; in both cases, we are only interested in certain “nice” amalgams, but the collection of nice amalgams have certain extendibility, continuity, and/or uniqueness properties which is needed for the analysis. This idea of selecting amalgams also underlies the body of literature concerning amalgamation of independent sets/types/diagrams, which arguably began with Shelah’s definition of the Dimensional Order Property in
[
She82
]
, and has developed in multiple directions (including the study of the homology of such diagrams by Goodrick, Kim, and Kolesnikov in
[
JK13
]
and the extension to AECs by Shelah and Vasey in
[
SV18
]
).

This selection of certain amalgams as “nice” is of course a common feature in algebra, such as the construction of free amalgams of groups or direct sums of modules. In a more general setting, Eklof presented in
[
Ekl08
]
an abstract notion of “freeness” for a class of modules (building on Shelah’s singular compactness theorem from
[
She75
]
and further work by Hodge in
[
Hod81
]
); this class of “free” models is characterised by each model having an associated “basis” which are extended under strong embeddings. Whilst this is a powerful abstraction from an algebraic point of view, this is somewhat problematic model-theoretically because it presumes that each model is generated by its basis, and “generation” translates poorly to non-algebraic contexts. On the other hand, this notion of “freeness” can also be understood in terms of designating an amalgam
N
of models
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}
as a “free amalgam” iff there is a basis of
N
which is the union of a basis of
M_{1}
and a basis of
M_{2}
which agree on
M_{0}
. Doing so allows us to focus on the operation of amalgamating models instead of studying their bases, which bypasses the problem of what “generation” should mean.

Building on this idea, in this paper we present a framework of a “notion of amalgamation” for a given AEC. Abstracting from the examples of free amalgamation of groups and direct sum of modules, we isolate the axiomatic properties of absolute minimality, regularity, continuity, and admitting decomposition (Definition
2.1
), which we assume throughout the paper. We also define the uniqueness property of amalgams, which intuitively states that for any triple of models there is a unique amalgam (up to isomorphism) which is “nice”. We refer to a notion satisfying all of the above as a notion of free amalgamation, and establish that when a class
K
has a notion of free amalgamation and is categorical in a sufficiently large cardinal, then it behaves analogously to the models of a unidimensional first order theory. This allows us to prove a categoricity transfer theorem (Theorem
5.6
):

Theorem
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
, and
K
has a prime and minimal model. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical in some
\lambda\geq\theta(K)
, then
K
is
\kappa
-categorical in every cardinal
\kappa\geq\theta(K)+(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}
.

(In this formulation, the cardinal
\theta(K)
is defined from the given notion of free amalgamation, and is analogous to
\kappa(T)
for
T
a simple theory.)

Of course, this begs the question of how strong the assumptions above are. In particular, we have mentioned previously that the assumption of unique “nice” amalgams implies that types have unique nonforking extensions. In fact, like stability, the uniqueness property delineates between structural results on one hand and anti-structural results on the other. This can be seen by combining the above theorem and Theorem
6.8
:

Theorem
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal and has weak 3-existence. If
(M_{b},M^{*},M)
is a non-uniqueness triple and
p=\text{gtp}(M^{*}/M_{b},M^{*})
, then there is
N\geq M
such that
p
has
2^{|N|}
-many extensions to
N
.

To tie all of this back to Grossberg and Lessman’s investigation of pregeometries, we would like to see how our results apply to a class with pregeometries. In particular, we consider a type
p
with
U(p)=1
and
K_{p}
the class of realizations of
p
: this class (under some assumptions) is naturally associated with corresponding pregeometries, which allows us to conclude (Theorem
7.13
):

Theorem
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersections and has a stable independence relation with the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property. If
U(p)=1
, then
K_{p}
is
\lambda
-categorical in all
\lambda>|\text{dom }p|+\+LS(K)

Notably, this is analogous to the case of an uncountably categorical countable theory, where the sets
\phi(M)
for a strongly minimal
\phi(x)
are also uncountably categorical. This is, of course, a crucial component of the Baldwin-Lachlan proof of Morley’s categoricity theorem.

The outline of this paper is as follows: in section 1, we formally define notions of amalgamation for an abstract class, and establish some basic properties which follow from the definition. We introduce some axiomatic properties for notions of amalgamation in section 2, and also explore both examples and counter-examples to these properties.

Section 3 introduces sequential amalgamation, and most of the section is dedicated to proving Theorem
3.14
, which roughly states that when
\mathscr{A}
is well-behaved, then the ordering of the sequence of amalgamation does not affect the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam. We also introduce some notation for amalgams and the cardinal invariant
\mu(K)
. These notions are used in section 4, where an independence relation is defined based on a given notion of amalgamation, and we show that this independence relation behaves similarly to forking in a (super)stable theory.

Section 5 uses the additional assumption that
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness (as well as some other axiomatic properties introduced in section 2) to show that the class
K
admits categoricity transfer at cardinals
>\theta(K)+2^{\text{LS}(K)}
, where
\theta(K)
is a cardinal characteristic derived from the notion of amalgamation. On the other hand, in section 6 we show that failing to have uniqueness implies that there are arbitrarily large models with the maximal number of non-isomorphic (in fact non-biembeddable) extensions. Finally, in section 7 we apply the technology developed to the class
K_{p}
, which are the realizations of some type
p
with
U(p)=1
, and show that
K_{p}
is necessarily categorical in a tail of cardinals.

This paper was written during the author’s Ph.D. program at Carnegie Mellon University. I would like to thank my advisor Rami Grossberg for his feedback and support for this project, and Marcos Mazari Armida for many useful discussions.

0.
Preliminaries

We first recall some basic definitions regarding abstract elementary classes (AECs) which are found in the literature. A more detailed overview of basic concepts and results can be found in
[
Bal09
]
.

Definition 0.1
.

Let
\tau
be a language.

(1)

(K,\leq_{K})
is an
abstract class
(in
\tau
) iff:

•

K
is a class of
\tau
-structures which are closed under
\tau
-isomorphisms

•

\leq_{K}
is a partial order on
K
, and
M\leq N
implies that
M
is a
\tau
-substructure of
N

•

The partial order is invariant under isomorphisms: if
M\leq_{K}N
,
M^{\prime}\subseteq N^{\prime}
,
f:M\simeq M^{\prime}
and
g:N\simeq N^{\prime}
are isomorphisms, and
f\subseteq g
, then
M^{\prime}\leq_{K}N^{\prime}

(2)

(K,\leq_{K})
is a
very weak abstract elementary class
if it is an abstract class that satisfies:

•

The
Löwenheim-Skolem property
: there is a cardinal
\text{LS}(K)
such that for any model
N\in K
and any set
A\subseteq N
, there is
M\leq_{K}N
such that
A\subseteq M
and
|M|\leq|A|+\text{LS}(K)

•

The
(weak) Tarski-Vaught chain property
: if
\alpha
is a limit ordinal and
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is an
\leq_{K}
-increasing continuous chain of models in
K
, then
N:=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}
is also a model in
K
, and each
M_{i}\leq_{K}N

(3)

(K,\leq_{K})
is an
weak abstract elementary class
if it is a very weak AEC which additionally satisfies the
Coherence property
: if
M_{1}\leq_{K}N
,
M_{2}\leq_{K}N
, and
M_{1}\subseteq M_{2}
, then
M_{1}\leq_{K}M_{2}

(4)

(K,\leq_{K})
is an
abstract elementary class
if it is a weak AEC which additionally satisfies the
Smoothness property
: if
\alpha
is a limit ordinal,
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is an
\leq_{K}
-increasing continuous chain, and for each
i<\alpha
we have that
M_{i}\leq_{K}N
, then
M_{\alpha}:=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}\in K
and
M_{\alpha}\leq_{K}N

For
(K,\leq_{K})
an abstract class, we denote by
\tau(K)
the language of the models in
K
. We drop the subscript in
\leq_{K}
when it is clear from context.

Definition 0.2
.

Let
(K,\leq)
be an abstract class.

(1)

Given
M,N\in K
, a
\tau
-homomorphism
f:M\longrightarrow N
is a
K
-embedding
iff
f
is a
\tau
-isomorphism between
M
and
f[M]
, and
f[M]\leq N

(2)

(K,\leq)
has the
Amalgamation Property
(AP) if for models
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2}
with
K
-embeddings
f_{1}:M_{0}\longrightarrow M_{1},f_{2}:M_{0}\longrightarrow M_{2}
, there is a model
N\in K
with
K
-embeddings
g_{1}:M_{1}\longrightarrow N,g_{2}:M_{2}\longrightarrow N
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

(3)

We define the class
K^{3}:=\{(\bar{a},M,N):M\leq N,\bar{a}\in N\}

(4)

Given
(\bar{a}_{1},M,N_{1}),(\bar{a}_{2},M,N_{2})\in K^{3}
, we define the relation
\sim
such that
(\bar{a}_{1},M,N_{1})\sim(\bar{a}_{2},M,N_{2})
iff there is a model
N^{\prime}\geq N_{2}
and a
K
-embedding
f:N_{1}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
such that
f\upharpoonright M=\text{id}_{M}
and
f(\bar{a}_{1})=\bar{a}_{2}

Fact 0.3
.

If
(K,\leq)
has AP, then
\sim
is an equivalence relation.

Definition 0.4
.

Given
(\bar{a},M,N)\in K^{3}
, the
Galois type

\text{gtp}(\bar{a}/M,N)
is the equivalence class of
(\bar{a},M,N)
under
\sim
. We say that
\bar{a}
realizes the Galois type
p
if
\text{gtp}(\bar{a}/M,N)=p
. Given an ordered set
I
, we let
S^{I}(M)
denote the collection of Galois types of the form
\text{gtp}((a_{i})_{i\in I}/M,N)

1.
Notions of Amalgamation

Let
(K,\leq)
be an abstract class. We would like to capture the idea of selecting certain amalgams of triples
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}
and designating them as the “nice” amalgams that we will focus on; this is formalized in the following definition.

Definition 1.1
.

Let the tuple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
be given such that
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2}\in K
,
M_{0}\leq M_{1}
and
f:M_{0}\longrightarrow M_{2}
is a
K
-embedding. A triple
(N,g_{1},g_{2})
is an
amalgam of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
over
M_{0}
via
f
if
N\in K
,
g_{1}:M_{1}\longrightarrow N
and
g_{2}:M_{2}\longrightarrow N
are
K
-embeddings, and the following diagram commutes (where
\iota
denotes the inclusion embedding):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

For simplicity, we will also refer to the above diagram as an amalgam (of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
over
M_{0}
via
f
). We denote the collection of such amalgams by
\text{Amal}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
.

A (class) function
\mathscr{A}
is a
pre-notion of amalgamation
if:

•

Its domain is the class of tuples
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
such that
M_{0}\leq M_{1}
and
f:M_{0}\longrightarrow M_{2}
is a
K
-embedding; and

•

For each such tuple,
\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)\subseteq\text{Amal}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)

For a triplet
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
, we say that
(N,g_{1},g_{2})
is an

\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
over
M_{0}
via
f
, which we will also denote by the annotated diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

We say that
\mathscr{A}
is a
notion of amalgamation
if in addition to being a pre-notion, the following properties hold of
\mathscr{A}
:

•

(Completeness) For every tuple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
as above,
\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
is nonempty.

•

\mathscr{A}
contains trivial amalgams: For any
M_{0}\leq M_{1}
,
(M_{1},\iota,\text{id})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{0},M_{1},\iota)
. Diagrammatically,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
id
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

•

(Top Invariance) For every
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
and every
K
-isomorphism
h:N\simeq N^{\prime}
,
(N^{\prime},h\circ g_{1},h\circ g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
. Diagrammatically,

\hbox to146.32pt{\vbox to50.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 73.15962pt\lower-24.99504pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
and
K
-isomorphism
h:M_{1}\simeq M^{\prime}
,
(N,g_{1}\circ h^{-1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(h[M_{0}],M^{\prime},M_{2},f\circ(h\upharpoonright M_{0})^{-1})

(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
and
K
-isomorphism
h:M_{2}\simeq M^{\prime}
,

(Symmetry) If
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
, then
(N,g_{2},g_{1})\in\mathscr{A}(f[M_{0}],M_{2},M_{1},f^{-1})
. Diagrammatically,

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{24.3003pt}{0.08134pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Remark
.

Technically,
\mathscr{A}
fails to even be a class function in the strictest sense, as
\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
is a proper class because of the invariance properties and also because there is no bound on the cardinality of the amalgams. This can of course be resolved by the assumption of a strongly inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
such that every model of
K
has cardinality
<\kappa
; in any case, this is inconsequential to this paper.

Clearly, if
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation for
K
, then
K
must have the Amalgamation Property as
\mathscr{A}
is complete. Generally, we are interested in notions of amalgamation which specify certain well-behaved amalgams: for example, if
K
has the Disjoint Amalgamation Property, we may define
\mathscr{A}_{d}
as only the amalgams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

where
g_{1}[M_{1}]\cap g_{2}[M_{2}]=g_{1}[M_{0}]
. Since we would like to work in
K
while ignoring the other amalgams which are not well-behaved, the properties defined above are designed such that some basic results which hold for amalgamation in general also hold for
\mathscr{A}
. For example:

Lemma 1.2
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation, and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

(1)

There is some
N^{\prime}\geq M_{1}
and
g_{2}^{\prime}:M_{2}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(2)

There is some
N^{\prime\prime}\geq M_{2}
and
g_{1}^{\prime}:M_{1}\longrightarrow N^{\prime\prime}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}^{\prime}}

Proof.

(1)

Let
N^{\prime}
be a copy of
N
such that
M_{1}\leq N^{\prime}
, and
h:N\simeq N^{\prime}
be such that
h\circ g_{1}=\iota:M_{1}\hookrightarrow N^{\prime}
. Letting
g_{2}^{\prime}=h\circ g_{2}
, the desired result follows from Top Invariance.

(2)

Similar to (1), using
N^{\prime\prime}
a copy of
N
such that
M_{2}\leq N^{\prime\prime}
.

∎

Lemma 1.3
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation, and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

Then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}[M_{2}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proof.

Firstly, note that as the diagram is commutative, indeed
g_{1}[M_{0}]=(g_{2}\circ f)[M_{0}]\leq g_{2}[M_{2}]
. By Side Invariance 1 (via the isomorphism
g_{1}:M_{1}\simeq g_{1}[M_{1}]
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ(g_{1}\upharpoonright M_{0})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Then, by Side Invariance 2 (via the isomorphism
g_{2}:M_{2}\simeq g_{2}[M_{2}]
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}[M_{2}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}[M_{1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}\circ f\circ(g_{1}\upharpoonright M_{0})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Finally, as
g_{1}\upharpoonright M_{0}=g_{2}\circ f
, hence
g_{2}\circ f\circ(g_{1}\upharpoonright M_{0})^{-1}=\iota:g_{1}[M_{1}]\hookrightarrow g_{2}[M_{2}]
as desired.
∎

Given the above lemmas, we see that to specify a notion of amalgamation
\mathscr{A}
, it suffices to specify when a commutative square of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

is in fact a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam. Similarly, for most results of
\mathscr{A}
, it suffices to prove the statement only for commutative diagrams as above.

Remark
.

Within the model theory literature, it is customary to say that
N
is an amalagam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
if there is a
K
-embedding
f
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Hence, if
N
is an amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
, then for any
N^{\prime}\geq N
, in this customary language it is also true that
N^{\prime}
is an amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
. On the other hand, in this paper the phrase “
N
(with
g_{1},g_{2}
) is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
(via
f
)” refers specifically to the statement “
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
”. In particular, since we do not assume that
\mathscr{A}
has any upward-closure property, it is not necessarily true that for every
N^{\prime}\geq N
,
(N^{\prime},g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
. It is, however, a relevant concept for the current investigation, and so we introduce a slight variant of the phrase to differentiate this interesting case:

Definition 1.4
.

We say that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam by inclusion
of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
over
M_{0}
if the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

For
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
, we say that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
if there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam by inclusion of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
.

Remark
.

It is important to note that we are
not
asserting that every triple
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}
can be amalgamated by inclusions; nor will we be assuming that such a property holds for any notion of amalgamation we consider. This definition simply allows us to refer specifically to
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams of the above form.

Example 1.5
.

Let
K
be the class of vector spaces over a fixed field
F
, with
\leq_{K}
the subspace relation. We can define
\mathscr{A}
such that for
V\leq W_{1},W_{2}\leq U
,
U
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
W_{1},W_{2}
over
V
(by inclusion) iff
W_{1}\cap W_{2}=V
and
\text{span}(W_{1}\cup W_{2})=U
. In this example, if
U^{\prime}\gneq U
, then
U^{\prime}
is
not
an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
W_{1},W_{2}
over
V
. However,
W_{1},W_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
V
inside
U^{\prime}
. More generally, if
T
is (for example) a first order stable theory, we can define
\mathscr{A}
such that for models
M_{0}\preccurlyeq M_{1},M_{2}
with
M_{1}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}M_{2}
,
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
amalgam iff
N
is
(a,\kappa_{r}(T))
-prime over
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
.

Some other examples of notions of amalgamation which we are interested in include:

(1)

Consider the class of groups with the subgroup ordering. Given
G\leq H,K
, the free amalgamated product
H\ast_{G}K
is formed by taking the free product of
H,K
and identifying the two copies of
G
together. This defines a notion of amalgamation on the class.

(2)

Similarly, consider the class of (left-) modules over a fixed ring
R
with the submodule ordering. As in the case for vector spaces, we can define
\mathscr{A}
such that given
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
,
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
iff
M_{1}\cap M_{2}=M_{0}
and
\text{span}(M_{1}\cup M_{2})=N
. Note that this is equivalent to defining
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams by taking direct sums and quotienting to identify the copies of the amalgamation base.

(3)

Consider the class of algebraically closed fields with characteristic
p
: Given
K_{0}\leq K_{1},K_{2}\leq L
, we define
\mathscr{A}
such that
L
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
K_{1},K_{2}
over
K_{0}
iff
K_{1}\cap K_{2}=K_{0}
,
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are algebraically independent over
K_{0}
, and
\text{acl}(K_{1}\cap K_{2})=L
. More generally, this construction holds for any AEC
K
where each model has a pregeometry which is “coherent” with
K
; we will develop this idea further in section 7.

(4)

In a different vein, let
K
be a class of algebras which is an expansion of Boolean algebras, for example the class of cylindric algebras or polyadic algebras (of some fixed dimension
\alpha
).
K
is said to have the
super amalgamation property
if any span
A_{0},A_{1},A_{2}
can be amalgamated by some
(B,f_{1},f_{2})
satisfying: for every
x\in A_{1}
and
y\in A_{2}
, if
f_{1}(x)\leq f_{2}(y)
then there is
z\in A_{0}
such that
x\leq z
and
z\leq y
, and vice versa. If
K
has the super amalgamation property, then the super amalgams define a notion of amalgamation.

(5)

In
[
SV18
]
, the notion of
\phi
-amalgamation is defined over an
AEC
for a quantifier-free formula
\phi
(assuming for simplicity that the language
\tau
is relational): the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

is a
\phi
-amalgam iff
\phi(M_{1}),\phi(M_{2})
are equal as
\tau
-structures and
f_{1}\upharpoonright\phi(M_{1})=f_{2}\upharpoonright\phi(M_{2})
. This is clearly also a notion of amalgamation in the current sense.

(6)

On the other hand, in the class of groups with the subgroup ordering, we can define another notion
\mathscr{A}
such that for
G_{0}\leq G_{1},G_{2}\leq H
,
H
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},G_{2}
over
G_{0}
iff
H=\langle G_{1}\cup G_{2}\rangle
. This is an example where
\mathscr{A}
gives very little structural information about the class.

2.
Some Structural Properties

The last example above shows that even with
\mathscr{A}
a specifically defined notion of amalgamation,
\mathscr{A}
might not provide any structural information on the underlying class besides having the amalgamation property. As we are interested in stronger results which do not follow simply from the fact that
K
has AP, we are interested in notions which satisfy some extra properties.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
K
be an abstract class, and let
\mathscr{A}
be a notion of amalgamation in
K
.

•

\mathscr{A}
is
minimal
if for every
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
,
N
is minimal over
g_{1}[M_{1}]\cup g_{2}[M_{2}]
i.e. if
N^{\prime}\leq N
and
g_{1}[M_{1}]\cup g_{2}[M_{2}]\subseteq N^{\prime}
, then
N^{\prime}=N
.

•

\mathscr{A}
is
absolutely minimal
if for every
(N,g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
and for any
N^{*}\geq N
, if
N^{\prime}\leq N^{*}
is such that
g_{1}[M_{1}]\cup g_{2}[M_{2}]\subseteq N^{\prime}
, then
N\leq N^{\prime}
.

•

\mathscr{A}
is
regular
if for every commutative square in
K
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The commutative square is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

(2)

There is some
M^{\prime},N^{\prime},g^{\prime}
such that
M_{0}\leq M^{\prime}\leq M_{1}
,
g_{2}[M_{2}]\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
, and
g^{\prime}=g_{1}\upharpoonright M^{\prime}
, with both of the following commutative squares being
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

(3)

For every
M^{\prime}
such that
M_{0}\leq M^{\prime}\leq M_{1}
, there exists
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that the following commutative square is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}\upharpoonright M^{\prime}}

Moreover, for any such choice of
N^{\prime}
, the following commutative square is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}\upharpoonright M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

•

\mathscr{A}
is
continuous
if for any limit
\delta
and increasing continuous chains
(M_{i})_{i<\delta},(N_{i})_{i<\delta}
with
K
-embeddings
(f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i})_{i<\delta}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

The commutative square of the respective unions is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}f_{i}}

•

\mathscr{A}

admits decompositions
if for every
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq N
, there is a
M_{2}
such that
M_{0}\leq M_{2}\leq N
and
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
(via the inclusion maps).

•

\mathscr{A}
has
uniqueness
if for any two amalgams
(N,g_{1},g_{2}),(N^{\prime},g_{1}^{\prime},g_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{A}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},f)
, there exists a
K
-
isomorphism

h:N\cong N^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}^{\prime}}

Remark
.

•

The “absolute” in “absolutely minimal” refers to the fact that the amalgam
N
of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
is minimal over
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
only relative to models
N^{\prime}
which can be jointly embedded with
N
; this is only an issue in the current framework since we do not assume the existence of monster models.

•

The literature is unfortunately split over the nomenclature for what is defined as uniqueness above: this property is sometimes known as “strong uniqueness”, whereas (using the language of
[
SV18
]
) “uniqueness” would refer to the property that two amalgamation diagrams can be amalgamated as indexed system of models. However, it is our opinion that within the current presentation the unqualified name “uniqueness” is more natural in terms of the existence of isomorphisms.

•

Furthermore, the uniqueness property is substantially different from the other properties defined above. This is because the properties such as minimality, continuity, and regularity are necessary for
\mathscr{A}
to resemble taking direct sums enough to motivate any further work (as we will discuss in Section 3). On the other hand, both the uniqueness property and its failure have significant model-theoretical consequences; we will explore the consequences of the positive case in Section 5, and the consequences of the negative case in Section 6.

With these properties, we can start differentiating between various notions of amalgamation and the implications on the structure of the underlying class. A simple but illustrative example comes from abelian groups, and more specifically the torsion divisible groups:

Example 2.2
.

Fix
S
a family of abelian groups such that for
G,H\in S
with
G\neq H
, for any abelian group
K
and group embeddings
f:G\longrightarrow K
,
g:H\longrightarrow K
,
f[G]\cap g[H]=0
, where
0
is the trivial group (for example, the Prufer
p
-groups
S:=\{\mathbb{Z}(p^{\infty}):p\text{ a prime}\}
). Define the class
K
such that
M\in K
iff
M
is a direct sum
\bigoplus_{i<\alpha}G^{\prime}_{i}
, where each
G^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic to some
G_{i}\in S
, and let the ordering
\leq_{K}
be the subgroup ordering. Note that the condition on
S
implies that if
G,H\in K
and
G\leq_{K}H
, then
H=G\oplus(\bigoplus_{i<\alpha}H^{\prime}i)
for some sequence of subgroups
H^{\prime}_{i}
which are isomorphic to groups in
S
.

In this case,
K
has an obvious notion of amalgamation
\mathscr{A}
, where
H
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G^{1},G^{2}
over
G^{0}
(by inclusion) iff
H=\bigoplus_{i<\alpha}H_{i}
, and there are sets
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq\alpha
such that:

•

For
l=0,1,2
,
G^{l}=\bigoplus_{i\in S_{l}}H_{i}

•

S_{1}\cap S_{2}=S_{0}
and
S_{1}\cup S_{2}=\alpha

It is straightforward to see that
\mathscr{A}
is minimal, absolutely minimal, regular, continuous, admits decomposition, and has uniqueness.

It is interesting to note that
S
as defined above cannot contain
\mathbb{Q}
since
\mathbb{Q}
can be embedded as a proper subgroup of itself. Of course, in the case where
K
is the class of divisible groups, since any divisible group admits a unique decomposition into copies of
Z(p^{\infty})
and
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathscr{A}
can be naturally extended to a notion of amalgamation in the class of divisible groups. In particular, this extension of
\mathscr{A}
formally relies on the natural notion of amalgamation in the class of vector spaces over
\mathbb{Q}
, which obviously satisfies all of the above properties. In this case, the notion
\mathscr{A}
on the class of divisible groups also satisfies all of these properties.

The free product over groups also gives rise to more complicated examples of amalgamation, for example using small cancellation theory:

Example 2.3
.

Let
S
be a class function on triples of groups, such that for
G_{0}\leq G_{1},G_{2}
,
S(G_{0},G_{1},G_{2})\subseteq\mathscr{P}(G_{1}\ast_{G_{0}}G_{2})
is a nonempty family of sets such that each
R\in S(G_{0},G_{1},G_{2})
is symmetrized and satisfies
C^{\prime}(1/6)
, where
C^{\prime}(\lambda)
is the metric small cancellation condition (see
[
Lyn
]
, Chapter 5 for discussion related to small cancellation theory, including the relevant definitions).

Now, let
K
be the class of groups ordered by the subgroup relation, and define
\mathscr{A}
such that given
G_{0}\leq G_{1},G_{2}\leq H
,
H
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},G_{2}
over
G_{0}
(by inclusion) iff
H\cong G_{1}\ast_{G_{0}}G_{2}/\langle R\rangle_{N}
, where
R\in S(G_{0},G_{1},G_{2})
and
\langle R\rangle_{N}
is the normal closure of
R
in
H
.

In particular, we note that if
S(G_{0},G_{1},G_{2})
contains (for example) both the empty set and a set not contained inside
G_{0}
, then there are two
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams of
G_{1},G_{2}
over
G_{0}
which are not isomorphic over
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
, and hence
\mathscr{A}
does not have uniqueness. Similarly, whether or not
\mathscr{A}
satisfies regularity, continuity, and admission of decomposition depends on the function
S
. On the other hand,
\mathscr{A}
is necessarily absolutely minimal as
H
is generated by
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
.

For the rest of this paper, we will restrict our attention to weak AECS:

Hypothesis 2.4
.

(K,\leq)
is a weak AEC.

We note, however, that many of the results presented do not require the Coherence property of weak AECs (Definition
0.1
), and so we will be explicit when using the Coherence property. On the other hand, the properties defined in Definition
2.1
for a notion of amalgamation puts additional constraints on the class, and the example below shows that even a very “natural” notion of amalgamation in a very weak AEC can fail to have the above properties:

Example 2.5
.

Consider the class
(K_{ACFp},\leq_{K})
, where
K_{ACFp}
is the class of algebraically closed fields of characteristic
p
but
L_{1}\leq_{K}L_{2}
iff
|L_{1}|<|L_{2}|
or
L_{2}
is a limit model over
L_{1}
. It is straightforward to check that
(K_{ACFp},\leq_{K})
is a very weak AEC. Note that
L_{2}
is a limit model over
L_{1}
iff
\text{td}(L_{2}/L_{1})=|L_{2}|
, where
\text{td}(K/F)
is the transcendental degree of
K
over
F
.

We define a notion of amalgamation
\mathscr{A}
in the following manner: given
L_{0}\leq_{K}L_{1},L_{2}\leq_{K}M
,
M
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
L_{1},L_{2}
over
L_{0}
(by inclusion) iff

(1)

L_{1}\cap L_{2}=L_{0}

(2)

L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are algebrically independent over
L_{0}

(3)

Assuming WLOG
|L_{1}|\leq|L_{2}|
,
\text{td}(M/L_{3})=|L_{2}|-|L_{1}|
, where
L_{3}:=\text{acl}(L_{1}\cup L_{2})

The third condition is necessary (for example) in the case where
|L_{1}|<|L_{2}|
, since in this case

\text{td}(L_{3}/L_{2})=\text{td}(L_{1}/L_{0})=|L_{1}|<|L_{2}|

which implies that
L_{3}
is not a limit model over
L_{2}
. On the other hand,
\mathscr{A}
does not satisfy some of the above properties:

•

\mathscr{A}
is not minimal: given models
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},M
as above, there is some model
M^{\prime}\lneq_{K}M
such that
|M^{\prime}|=|M|
and
L_{3}\leq_{K}M^{\prime}
, so in particular
\text{td}(M^{\prime}/L_{3})=\text{td}(M/L_{3})
. Hence
M^{\prime}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
L_{1},L_{2}
over
L_{0}
. This also shows that
\mathscr{A}
is not absolutely minimal (see also Lemma
2.11
).

•

\mathscr{A}
is not continuous: Suppose the set
\{a_{i}:i<\omega\}\cup\{b_{j}:j<\omega_{1}\}\subseteq M
are algebraically independent, and define:

(1)

M_{0}=\bar{\mathbb{Q}}

(2)

N_{0}=M_{0}(a_{i}:i<\omega)

(3)

For
\alpha\geq 1
,
M_{i}=M_{0}(b_{j}:j<\omega\cdot\alpha)

(4)

For
\alpha\geq 1
,
N_{i}=M_{0}(\{a_{i}:i<\omega\}\cup\{b_{j}:j<\omega\cdot\alpha\})

Note then this gives
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

On the other hand,
N_{\omega_{1}}=M_{0}(\{a_{i}:i<\omega\}\cup\{b_{j}:j<\omega_{1}\})
is not a limit model over
M_{\omega_{1}}=M_{0}(b_{j}:j<\omega_{1})
as
\text{td}(N_{\omega_{1}}/M_{\omega_{1}})=\aleph_{0}
, and so in particular
N_{\omega_{1}}
is not an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{0},M_{\omega_{1}}
over
M_{0}
.

By assuming that
\mathscr{A}
satisfies some of the properties from Definition
2.1
, a few basic results can be deduced. In particular, these results are analogous to basic properties of the direct sum on vector spaces.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation that is regular. If
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},M_{3},M^{\prime},N
are models such that:

(1)

M^{\prime}
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(2)

N
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{3},M^{\prime}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Then there is
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that:

(1)

N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{2},M_{3}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion; and

(2)

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},N^{\prime}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion

Proof.

Note that by the regularity, since
M_{0}\leq M_{2}\leq M^{\prime}
, there exists
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

In particular, we have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Applying regularity to the two commutative squares on the right, this shows that
N
is indeed a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{\prime},M_{1}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation and is absolutely minimal. If
M_{1},M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
, then there is a unique
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
N^{\prime}
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion.

Proof.

Let
N^{\prime}\leq N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, and suppose
N^{*}\leq N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion. In particular, hence
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\subseteq N^{*}
. As
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal and
N^{\prime},N^{*}\leq N
, hence
N^{\prime}\leq N^{*}
. The symmetric argument also shows that
N^{*}\leq N^{\prime}
, and hence
N^{\prime}=N^{*}
.
∎

Notation 2.8
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, and the models
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
are such that
M_{1},M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
, then we denote the unique
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam inside
N
by
M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal and regular. Then for any
M\leq N
, the operation
\oplus_{M}^{N}
is commutative and associative where defined.

Proof.

That
\oplus_{M}^{N}
is commutative is from
\mathscr{A}
being symmetric. Associativity follows from Lemma
2.6
.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

We say an AEC
K

admits finite intersections
(abbreviated to has
FI
) if whenever
M_{1},M_{2}
are such that there exists
M_{0},N
with
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
, then the intersection
M_{1}\cap M_{2}
is a model in
K
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a notion of amalgamation.

(1)

If
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, then it is minimal.

(2)

If
K
admits finite intersections and
\mathscr{A}
is minimal, then
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal.

Proof.

Note that by the Invariance properties of
\mathscr{A}
, it suffices to show that the above statements hold for any
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},N
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(1)

Assume that
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal. If
N^{\prime}\leq N
is such that
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\subseteq N^{\prime}
, then
N\leq N^{\prime}
by absolute minimality, and hence
N^{\prime}=N
. This shows that
\mathscr{A}
is minimal.

(2)

Assume that
K
admits finite intersections and
\mathscr{A}
is minimal. Then, if
N^{*}\geq N
and
N^{\prime}\leq N^{*}
is such that
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\subseteq N^{\prime}
, since
K
admits finite intersection,
N^{\prime\prime}=N\cap N^{\prime}
is also a model of
K
, and furthermore
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\subseteq N^{\prime\prime}
. But then by minimality,
N^{\prime\prime}=N
, and hence
N\leq N^{\prime}
as desired.

∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is minimal. If
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion and
|N|\geq\text{LS}(K)
, then
|N|=|M_{1}|+|M_{2}|+\text{LS}(K)
.

Proof.

Since
M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
, by the Löwenheim-Skolem axiom there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
|N^{\prime}|\leq\text{LS}(K)+|M_{1}\cup M_{2}|
. Since
\mathscr{A}
is minimal, hence
N^{\prime}=N
, giving the desired result.
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation that is regular and continuous. Let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},M
over
M_{b}
by inclusion,
\delta
be a limit ordinal, and
(M_{i})_{i<\delta}
be a continuous resolution of
M
such that
M_{b}\leq M_{0}
. Then there is a continuous resolution
(N_{i})_{i<\delta}
of
N
such that for each
i<\delta
,
N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},M_{i}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion.

Proof.

We will construct
N_{i}
by induction:

(1)

Since
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},M
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, and
M_{0}
is such that
M_{b}\leq M_{0}\leq M
, by regularity there is
N_{0}\leq N
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(2)

If
N_{i}
is already defined, by construction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

As
\mathscr{A}
is regular, it is also the case that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Since
M_{i}\leq M_{i+1}\leq M
, again by regularity, there is
N_{i+1}\leq N
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(3)

At limit stage
\alpha
, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

As
\mathscr{A}
is continuous and
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is an increasing continuous chain, letting
N_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}N_{i}
, we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

∎

3.
Sequential amalgamation

From a model-theoretic perspective, that the class of vector spaces over a fixed (countable) field is uncountably categorical stems from the exchange property of vectors and the fact that all vector spaces are direct sums of 1-dimensional spaces. In order to mimic this structure (or equivalently, the structure of models with a pregeometry), we must first define the amalgam of not only two models but of a possibly infinite sequence of models. We thus devote this section to showing that under the assumptions of
\mathscr{A}
being absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous, then sequential amalgamation under
\mathscr{A}
behaves as one would expect from the example of direct sums.

Notation 3.1
.

For an ordinal
\alpha
, we define the ordinal
s(\alpha)
by:

•

s(\alpha)=\alpha
for limit
\alpha

•

s(\alpha)=\alpha+1
otherwise

Definition 3.2
.

Let
M_{b}\in K
, and let
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
be a sequence of models such for each
i<\alpha
,
M_{b}\leq M_{i}
. We say that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
if there exists a sequence of models
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
and
K
-embeddings
(f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i+1})_{i<\alpha}
such that:

(1)

N_{0}=M_{b}
and
N_{1}=f_{0}[M_{0}]

(2)

For each
i<\alpha
,
f_{i}[M_{b}]=M_{b}
and
f_{i}[M_{i}]\leq N_{i+1}

(3)

(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
is a continuous resolution of
N
i.e. it is an increasing continuous chain with
N=\bigcup_{i<s(\alpha)}N_{i}
.

(4)

For every
i\geq 1
, the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

Paralleling the two-model case, we say that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam by inclusion
of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam as above with each
f_{i}
being an inclusion map
\iota_{i}:M_{i}\hookrightarrow N_{i+1}
. When each
M_{i}\leq N
, we say that
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{b}
inside
N
if there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam by inclusion.

In order to understand what properties of sequential amalgams is desirable for our analysis, recall that any divisible group can be uniquely decomposed as a direct sum of (copies of) the rationals and Prufer
p
-groups. Using this as a guiding example, ideally the amalgamation of a sequence of models should be independent from the order of amalgamation, and moreover it should be possible to take subsets of a “basis” to construct smaller models. In order to prove this claim (Theorem
3.14
), we proceed by a number of lemmas:

Lemma 3.3
.

If
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, then for any
\beta\leq\alpha
, there exists some
L\leq N
such that:

(1)

L
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\beta}
over
M_{b}
; and

(2)

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of the sequence
(L)^{\frown}(M_{i})_{\beta\leq i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}

Proof.

Let
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
be a continuous resolution of
N
witnessing that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
via the maps
(f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i+1})_{i<\alpha}
, and let
L=\bigcup_{i<\beta}N_{i}
. Then the resolution
(N_{i})_{i<s(\beta)}
witnesses that
L
is the desired
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam, and moreover the sequence
(L)^{\frown}(N_{i})_{\beta\leq i<s(\alpha)}
witnesses that
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(L)^{\frown}(M_{i})_{\beta\leq i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
(via the maps
(\iota:L\longrightarrow N)^{\frown}(f_{i})_{\beta\leq i<\alpha}
).
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation which is regular and continuous. If
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
via the maps
(f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N)_{i<\alpha}
, then there is some
L\leq N
such that:

•

L
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{1\leq i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
via the same maps; and

•

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
L
and
M_{0}
over
M_{b}
in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proof.

Fix
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
a continuous resolution of
N
witnessing that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
via
(f_{i})_{i<\alpha}
. Let us first construct the model
L
as the union of an increasing continuous chain
(L_{i})_{1\leq i<s(\alpha)}
, with the following conditions:

(1)

L_{1}=f_{1}[M_{1}]
, and each
L_{i}\leq N_{i}

(2)

For limit
\delta
,
L_{\delta}=\bigcup_{1\leq i<\delta}L_{i}

(3)

For
i\geq 1
, the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(4)

For
i\geq 1
, the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

For the successor step, recall that as
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
witnesses that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, in particular for each
i<\alpha
, the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Hence, as
L_{i}\leq N_{i}
by assumption, by regularity there exists some
L_{i+1}\leq N_{i+1}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

It remains to show that (4) is satisfied. We note that combining the above diagram and assuming (4) holds for
L_{i}
, we get the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

Applying regularity to the two commutative squares on the right, we see that (4) is satisfied at the
i+1
step:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

For the limit step, it suffices to check again that
L_{\delta}
satisfies (4). Since
L_{i}
satisfies (4) by assumption for
i<\delta
, we have the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}[M_{0}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Hence by continuity (and invariance), we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

This completes the definition of
(L_{i})_{1\leq i<s(\alpha)}
. Note then that this resolution of
L=\bigcup_{i<s(\alpha)}L_{i}
is a witness to the fact that
L
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{1\leq i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, and moreover the proof for (4) in the limit case also shows that
N=\bigcup_{i<s(\alpha)}N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{0},L
over
M_{b}
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular and continuous. If
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, then for any
0<j<\alpha
, there are
L_{1},L_{2}\leq N
such that:

•

L_{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<j}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion

•

L_{2}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{j<i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion; and

•

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(L_{1},M_{j},L_{2})
over
M_{b}
by inclusion

Proof.

That
L_{1}
exists by Lemma
3.3
and
L_{2}
exists by Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation that is regular and continuous. Let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},M^{\prime}
over
M_{b}
by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over

.

Proof.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

So let us define an increasing continuous chain

(3)

(4)
