Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i_{<}\alpha}
over
M_{b}
via the maps
(f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N)_{i<\alpha}
. If additionally
M_{0}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(L_{j})_{j<\beta}
over
M_{b}
via the maps
(g_{j}:L_{j}\longrightarrow M_{0})_{j<\beta}
, then
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of the concatenated sequence
(L_{j}:j<\beta)^{\frown}(M_{i}:i<\alpha)
over
M_{b}
.

Proof.

Fix
(M_{j}^{\prime})_{j<s(\beta)}
a continuous resolution of
M_{0}
witnessing that it is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(L_{j})_{j<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, and also fix
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
a continuous resolution of
N
witnessing that it is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
over
M_{b}
. Consider then the sequence
S=(f_{0}[M_{j}^{\prime}])_{j<s(\beta)}^{\frown}(N_{i}^{\prime})_{1\leq i<s(\alpha)}
: it is a continuous resolution of
N
since
N_{0}=f_{0}[M_{0}]=\bigcup_{j<s(\beta)}
. Since
f_{0}\upharpoonright M_{j}^{\prime}
is a
K
-isomorphism between
M_{j}^{\prime}
, and
f_{0}[M_{j}^{\prime}]
, Invariance of
\mathscr{A}
implies the desired result.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal. Let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, and suppose that
N^{\prime}\geq N
,
N^{*}\leq N^{\prime}
, and each
M_{i}\subseteq N^{*}
. Then
N\leq N^{*}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\alpha
:

•

If
\alpha=2
, then this is true by definition of
\mathscr{A}
being absolutely minimal.

•

Assuming the statement is true for
\alpha
. Given
N
an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha+1}
over
M_{b}
(by inclusion) and
N^{\prime},N^{*}
as above, let
N_{\alpha}\leq N
be such that
N_{\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, and hence by induction
N_{\alpha}\leq N^{*}
. But
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, and as
M_{\alpha}\leq N^{*}
by assumption, hence
N\leq N^{*}
as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal.

•

For limit
\delta
, if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\delta}
over
M_{b}
, then fix
(N_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\delta}
a continuous resolution of
N
witnessing that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam. In particular, each
N_{\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
. Now, given
N^{\prime},N^{*}
as above, by induction each
N_{\alpha}\leq N^{*}
. Since
N=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}N_{\alpha}
, hence
N\subseteq N^{*}
. Furthermore, as
N,N^{*}\leq N^{\prime}
, by Coherence we have that
N\leq N^{*}
.

∎

Corollary 3.9
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal. If each
M_{i}\leq N
and the sequence
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{b}
inside
N
, then there is a unique
N^{\prime}\leq N
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
.

Notation 3.10
.

If
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
are such that each
M_{b}\leq M_{i}\leq N
and the sequence
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated inside
N
via inclusion, then we denote the unique
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam inside
N
by
\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N}M_{i}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, and absolutely minimal. Let
\alpha
be a limit ordinal, and
(M^{1}_{i})_{i\leq\alpha},(M^{2}_{i})_{i\leq\alpha},(N_{i})_{i\leq\alpha}
be increasing continuous chains such that for each
i<\alpha
,
N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{i}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. Then for any
i,j<\alpha
, there is
M_{ij}\leq N_{\text{max}(i,j)}
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{j}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. Moreover, we can choose the system of
M_{ij}
’s such that if
i
is a limit ordinal, then
M_{ij}=\bigcup_{k<i}M_{kj}
, and similarly if
j
is a limit ordinal.

Proof.

Let
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{i},N_{i}
be as above, so that we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

where all the arrows are inclusions and all the commutative squares with a vertex at
M_{b}
are
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams. Letting
M_{ii}
be defined as
N_{i}
, we will define
M_{ij}
by induction on
\text{max}(i,j)<\alpha
such that in addition to the requirements above, we have additionally that the condition
(A(ij))
holds when
i,j
are not limits:

(A(i,j))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i-1,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i-1,j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,j-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(where
M_{-1,-1}=M_{b},M_{-1,j}=M^{2}_{j},M_{i,-1}=M^{1}_{i}
)

•

For
M_{01}
, note that
N_{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{1},M^{2}_{1}
over
M_{b}
(by inclusion). As
M_{b}\leq M^{1}_{0}\leq M^{1}_{1}
, by regularity there is
M_{01}\leq N_{1}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

M_{10}
is defined symmetrically.

•

If
M_{ij}
is defined for all
i,j\leq\alpha
, then for any
i\leq\alpha
, by regularity there is
M_{i,\alpha+1}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

It is straightforward to see that condition
(A(i,\alpha+1))
by induction on
i
(and using regularity for the base case). We define
M_{\alpha+1,j}
which satisfies
(A(\alpha+1,j))
by the symmetric argument. Finally, to see that condition
(A(\alpha+1,\alpha+1))
holds, note that by definition of
M_{\alpha,\alpha+1}
, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha,\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Apply regularity to the commutative square on the right (and symmetry), we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha+1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha,\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

The
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam on the right shows that
(A(\alpha+1,\alpha+1))
is indeed satisfied.

•

If
\delta
is a limit and
M_{ij}
are defined for
i,j<\delta
, then by regularity let
M_{i,\delta}\leq N_{\delta}
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{\delta}
over
M_{b}
. We need to show that:

Claim
.

M_{i,\delta}=\bigcup_{j<\delta}M_{ij}

To prove the claim, note that since
(M^{2}_{j})_{j<\delta}
is a continuous resolution of
M^{2}_{\delta}
, by Lemma
2.13
there is a continuous resolution
(M^{\prime}_{ij})_{j<\delta}
of
M_{i,\delta}
such that each
M^{\prime}_{ij}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{j}
over
M_{b}
. But then each
M^{\prime}_{ij},M_{ij}\leq N_{\delta}
, and as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, by Lemma
2.7
we have that
M_{ij}=M^{\prime}_{ij}
. This proves the claim. Additionally, this construction implies that when
\gamma
is also a limit, then
M_{\gamma,\delta}=\bigcup_{i<\gamma}M_{i,\delta}
.

Symmetrically, we define
M_{\delta,j}
. To finish the construction, we need to show that:

\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i,\delta}=N_{\delta}=\bigcup_{j<\delta}M_{\delta,j}

But this is true since each
N_{\alpha}\leq M_{\alpha,\delta},M_{\delta,\alpha}\leq N_{\delta}
, and
\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}N_{\alpha}=N_{\delta}
.

∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous. If
\alpha
is a limit ordinal, and
(M^{1}_{i})_{i\leq\alpha},(M^{2}_{i})_{i\leq\alpha},(N_{i})_{i\leq\alpha}
are increasing continuous chains such that for each
i<\alpha
,
N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{i},M^{2}_{i}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, then
N_{\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{\alpha},M^{2}_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
.

Proof.

For
i,j<\alpha
, let
M_{ij}\leq N_{\alpha}
be constructed as in the above Lemma, and for each
i<\alpha
, let
M_{i,\alpha}=\bigcup_{j<\alpha}M_{ij}
.

Claim
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proof.

Note that by condition
(A(0,j))
for each
j<\alpha
, we have that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Hence the claim holds as
\mathscr{A}
is continuous.
∎

Claim
.

For each
i<\alpha
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

This holds by the same argument.

Claim
.

For limit
\delta<\alpha
,
M_{\delta,\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i,\alpha}
, and moreover

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\delta,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proof.

Note that

\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i,\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\delta}\bigcup_{j<\alpha}M_{ij}=\bigcup_{j<\alpha}M_{\delta,j}=M_{\delta,\alpha}

For the moreover part, combining the above claims and induction along
\delta
, we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

As
\mathscr{A}
is continuous, hence
\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i,\alpha}=M_{\delta,\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{2}_{\alpha},M^{1}_{\delta}
over
M_{b}
.
∎

Combining the above claims, we get the diagram (for all
i<\alpha
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

As
\mathscr{A}
is continuous, hence
\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i,\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{1}_{\alpha},M^{2}_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
. But since for any
i<j<\alpha
,
N_{i}\leq M_{ij},M_{ji}\leq N_{j}
, we have that
N_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i,\alpha}
. This completes the proof.

Theorem 3.13
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous. Let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. Then for any subsequence
S\subseteq\alpha
, there is some
M_{S}\leq N
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(j)})_{j<|S|}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. Moreover, if
\bar{S}
is the complement of
S
in
\alpha
(and considered as an increasing sequence), then there is
M_{\bar{S}}
such that additionally,
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{S},M_{\bar{S}}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion.

Proof.

We will proceed by induction on the length of
\alpha
:

•

When
\alpha=2
, this is trivial.

•

Assume the claim holds for
\alpha
. Given
N
an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha+1}
over
M_{b}
, suppose that
S
is a subsequence of
\alpha+1
. This breaks down into three cases:

(1)

If
S=\{\alpha\}
, then the case is trivial.

(2)

If
S\subseteq\alpha
, then consider
N_{\alpha}\leq N
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
(as guaranteed by Lemma
3.3
): by the inductive hypothesis,
M_{S}\leq N_{\alpha}
exists, and so does
M_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}
, where
\bar{S}_{\alpha}
is the complement of
S
w.r.t.
\alpha
. Now, since
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{\alpha}
and
M_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, we get the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

By Lemma
2.6
, hence there is some
M_{\bar{S}}
such that:

–

M_{\bar{S}}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}},M_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
; and

–

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{S},M_{\bar{S}}
over
M_{b}

Furthermore, by Lemma
3.7
,
M_{\bar{S}}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{j}:j<|\bar{S}_{\alpha}|)^{\frown}(M_{\alpha})
over
M_{b}
.

(3)

If
S\ni\alpha
and
S\cap\alpha\neq\varnothing
, then
\bar{S}
satisfies the above case (2), so the same construction gives the required submodels.

•

Let
\delta
be a limit, and suppose the claim holds for all
\alpha<\delta
. Given
N
an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\delta}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, let
(N_{i})_{i<\delta}
be a continuous resolution of
N
such that each
N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{j})_{j<i}
over
M_{b}
. Now, if
S
is a subsequence of
\delta
, denote
S_{\alpha}:=S\upharpoonright\alpha
and
\bar{S}_{\alpha}:=\bar{S}\upharpoonright\alpha
. Note then that for each
\alpha
,
\bar{S}_{\alpha}
is the complement of
S_{\alpha}
relative to
\alpha
, and hence the inductive hypothesis implies that there are models
M^{\alpha}_{S_{\alpha}},M^{\alpha}_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}\leq N_{\alpha}
such that:

–

M^{\alpha}_{S_{\alpha}}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(j)})_{j<|S_{\alpha}|}
over
M_{b}

–

M^{\alpha}_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\bar{S}(j)})_{j<|\bar{S}_{\alpha}|}
over
M_{b}

–

N_{\alpha}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{\alpha}_{S_{\alpha}},M^{\alpha}_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}
over
M_{b}

Moreover, by Lemma
3.8
,
M^{\alpha}_{S_{\alpha}}
is the unique
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(j)})_{j<|S|}
over
M_{b}
inside
N_{\delta}
, and similarly for
M^{\alpha}_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}
. Hence we will drop the superscript, and define
M_{S}:=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}M_{S_{\alpha}},M_{\bar{S}}:=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}M_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}}
. Note then that the chains
(M_{S_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta},(M_{\bar{S}_{\alpha}})_{\alpha\leq\delta},(N_{\alpha})_{\alpha\leq\delta}
satisfies the hypothesis of Corollary
3.12
above, and hence
N_{\delta}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{S},M_{\bar{S}}
over
M_{b}
. Moreover, the continuous resolution
(M_{S_{\alpha}})_{\alpha<\delta}
witnesses that
M_{S}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(j)})_{j<|S|}
over
M_{b}
as desired.

∎

Remark
.

It should be noted that if
K
is assumed to be an AEC rather than a weak AEC (i.e.
K
has Smoothness), then the proof of the above theorem can be simplified considerably: if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion and
S
is a subsequence of
\alpha
, then the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(i)})_{i<\text{otp}(S)}
over
M_{b}
can be easily defined by induction. This works even at limit stages when
K
is assumed to have Smoothness; otherwise, the above argument seems to be necessary.

Theorem 3.14
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous. Let
\alpha\geq 2
be an ordinal, and
\sigma:|\alpha|\longrightarrow\alpha
be any enumeration of
\alpha
. Then
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion iff
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(j)})_{j<|\alpha|}
.

Proof.

Let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. We proceed by induction on
\alpha
:

•

When
\alpha=2
, this is just Lemma
2.9
.

•

Suppose the claim holds for
n
, which is finite. If
\sigma
is an enumeration of
n+1
, then
\sigma
is a permutation of
n+1
. There are two cases to consider:

–

If
\sigma(n)=n
, then
\sigma\upharpoonright n
is a permutation of
n
, and the claim follows from the inductive hypothesis.

–

Otherwise, let
m=\sigma(n)<n
. By Corollary
3.5
, there are models
N_{1},N_{2}\leq N
such that:

*

N_{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<m}
over
M_{b}

*

N_{2}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{m<i\leq n}
over
M_{b}

*

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(N_{1},M_{m},N_{2})
over
M_{b}

But then by Lemma
2.9
,
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(N_{1},N_{2},M_{m})
over
M_{b}
. Now, if
N^{\prime}\leq N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{1},N_{2}
over
M_{b}
, then by Lemma
3.7
and
3.6
,
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{j})_{j\neq m}
over
M_{b}
. Since
\sigma(n)=m
and
\sigma
is a permutation, (by re-indexing) the inductive hypothesis implies that
N^{\prime}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{i<n}
over
M_{b}
, and hence
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{i<n+1}
over
M_{b}
.

•

Suppose the claim holds for an infinite
\alpha
, and so
|\alpha|=|\alpha+1|
. Given
\sigma:|\alpha|\longrightarrow\alpha+1
an enumeration, there is some
\beta<|\alpha|
such that
\sigma(\beta)=\alpha
. Let
S
be the subsequence of
\alpha
such that
\text{ran }S=\text{ran }\sigma\upharpoonright\beta
, and let
\bar{S}
be its complement in
\alpha
, so in particular
\bar{S}=S^{\prime\frown}\alpha
for some subsequence
S^{\prime}
of
\alpha
. Now, since
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha+1}
over
M_{b}
, there is an
N^{*}\leq N
such that
N^{*}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, and
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{*},M_{\alpha}
over
M_{b}
. But since
S
is a subsequence of
\alpha
and
S^{\prime}
is its complement w.r.t.
\alpha
, by Theorem
3.13
there are models
N_{S},N_{S^{\prime}}\leq N^{*}
such that

–

N_{S}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S(i)})_{i<|S|}
over
M_{b}

–

N_{S^{\prime}}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S^{\prime}(i)})_{i<|S^{\prime}|}
over
M_{b}

–

N^{*}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{S},N_{S^{\prime}}
over
M_{b}

Furthermore, since
S,S^{\prime}
are subsequences of
\alpha
,
\text{otp}(S),\text{otp}(S^{\prime})\leq\alpha
, and so by the inductive hypothesis
N_{S}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{i<\beta}
over
M_{b}
. Similarly,
N_{S^{\prime}}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{\beta<i<|\alpha|}
. Moreover,
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(N_{S},M_{\alpha},N_{S}^{\prime})
over
M_{b}
by Lemma
2.9
, and so by Lemma
3.7
and
3.6
,
N
is indeed an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{i<|\alpha|}
over
M_{b}
.
We also need to show that if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<|\alpha|}
over
M_{b}
, then
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})_{j<\alpha+1}
. Again letting
\beta
be such that
\sigma(\alpha)=\beta
, by Lemma
3.5
there are models
N^{1},N^{2}\leq N
such that

–

N^{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\beta}
over
M_{b}

–

N^{2}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{\beta<i<|\alpha|}
over
M_{b}

–

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(N^{1},M_{\beta},N^{2})
over
M_{b}

By Lemma
2.9
again, we see that
N
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(N^{1},N^{2},M_{\beta})
over
M_{b}
. If
N^{\prime}\leq N
is such that
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{1},N^{2}
over
M_{b}
, then by the inductive hypothesis
N^{\prime}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})_{j<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
. By Lemma
3.7
, hence
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})_{j<\alpha+1}
over
M_{b}
.

•

Suppose
\alpha
is a limit ordinal, and that the claim holds for all
\beta<\alpha
. As
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
, let
(N_{\beta})_{\beta<\alpha}
be a continuous resolution of
N
such that each
N_{\beta}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\beta}
over
M_{b}
. Now, given
\sigma:|\alpha|\longrightarrow\alpha
an enumeration, for
j<|\alpha|
let
\sigma_{j}:=\sigma\upharpoonright j
, and let
S_{j}
be a subsequence of
\alpha
such that
\text{ran }S_{j}=\text{ran }\sigma_{j}
i.e.
S_{j}
is the set enumerated by
\sigma_{j}
but re-indexed by the ordinal ordering. Note that since each
S_{j}
is a subsequence of
\alpha
, by Theorem
3.13
there is
N_{S_{j}}\leq N
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{S_{j}(i)})_{i<\text{otp}(S_{j})}
over
M_{b}
. Furthermore, since each
|S_{j}|<|\alpha|
,
\text{otp}(S_{j})<\alpha
, and hence by the inductive hypothesis
N_{S_{j}}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma_{j}(i)})_{i<j}
over
M_{b}
. Letting
N^{\prime}=\bigcup_{j<|\alpha|}N_{S_{j}}
, this implies that
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma(i)})_{i<|\alpha|}
over
M_{b}
.

Claim
.

N^{\prime}=N

Proof.

Since
(N_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is a continuous resolution of
N
, it suffices to show that each
N_{i}\subseteq N^{\prime}
. Now, for each
i<\alpha
, let
\zeta_{i}
be a subsequence of
\sigma
such that
\text{ran }\zeta_{i}=i
, and so by the inductive hypothesis
N_{i}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\zeta_{i}(j)})_{j<\text{otp}(\zeta_{i})}
over
M_{b}
. But by Theorem
3.13
there is
N^{\prime}_{i}\leq N^{\prime}
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\zeta_{i}(j)})_{j<\text{otp}(\zeta_{i})}
over
M_{b}
, and as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal hence
N^{\prime}_{i}=N_{i}
. This proves the claim.
∎

It remains to show, that when
\alpha
is
not
an initial ordinal, that if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<|\alpha|}
over
M_{b}
, then it is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})_{j<\alpha}
. However, we note that the argument analogous to the one given above also works here, and hence the claim is proven for
\alpha
.

∎

Given Theorems
3.13
and
3.14
, we see that when
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous, then
\mathscr{A}
-amalgamation of models indexed by a sequence is independent of the ordering, and hence can be considered as being indexed by a set. Moreover, if
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i\in Y}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, then for any
X\subseteq Y
, there is
N_{X}\leq N
which is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i\in X}
over
M_{b}
.

Before moving on from sequential amalgamation, let us note that when
\mathscr{A}
is additionally assumed to admit decomposition, this actually allows a model to be decomposed as the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of a sequence of small models:

Lemma 3.15
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation which is regular and admits decomposition. Then for any
M_{b}\lneq N
, there exists an ordinal
\alpha<|N|^{+}
and a sequence of models
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
such that:

•

For every
i<\alpha
,
M_{b}\lneq M_{i}\leq N
and
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)+|M_{b}|

•

N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=|N|^{+}
and
\mu=|M_{b}|+\text{LS}(K)
. We will try to define two sequences of models,
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
and
(N_{i})_{i<\lambda}
, such that:

(1)

For each
i
,
M_{b}\leq M_{i}\leq N
,
N_{i}\leq N
, and
|M_{i}|=\mu

(2)

(N_{i})_{i<\lambda}
is an increasing continuous chain with
N_{0}=M_{b}
and
N_{1}=M_{0}

(3)

For every
i\geq 1
, the following diagram is an
\mathscr{A}
-diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proceeding inductively:

•

For
i=0
, let
M_{0}
be any model such that
M_{b}\lneq M_{0}\leq N
and
|M_{0}|=\mu
, and let
N_{0}=M_{b},N_{1}=M_{0}
.

•

Suppose inductively that
M_{i},N_{i+1}
has been defined to satisfy (3). Since
N_{i+1}\leq N
, either
N_{i+1}=N
or
N_{i+1}\lneq N
. In the former case, we terminate the inductive construction; otherwise, since
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition, there is some
M_{i+1}^{\prime}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Note that as
\mathscr{A}
is minimal,
M_{i+1}^{\prime}-N_{i+1}
must be nonempty as otherwise
N_{i+1}=N
. So let
M_{i+1}
be any model of cardinality
\mu
such that
M_{b}\lneq M_{i+1}\leq M_{i+1}^{\prime}
and
M_{i+1}-N_{i+1}
is nonempty. Then, as
M_{b}\leq M_{i+1}\leq M_{i+1}^{\prime}
, by regularity there exists some
N_{i+2}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

•

For limit
\delta
, let
N_{\delta}=\bigcup_{i<\delta}N_{i}
. If
N=N_{\delta}
, then the construction terminates; otherwise,
M_{\delta}
and
N_{\delta+1}
can be defined by the same procedure as in the successor case.

Note that by construction, each
N_{i}\lneq N_{i+1}\leq N
, and as
\lambda=|N|^{+}
, hence the above procedure must terminate at some ordinal
\alpha<\lambda
. In that case,
(N_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
witnesses the fact that
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion.
∎

One last but important property of the direct sum in vector spaces and divisible groups is that under any “basis” decomposition, any element is contained within the “span” (or amalgam) of finitely many basis elements. Whilst this is clearly true in the two examples because such algebraic objects are finitary, in the present context we are also interested in classes which are infinitary but not unboundedly; analogously, there are interesting classes which are
\mathscr{L}_{\kappa,\omega}
classes rather than just a
\mathscr{L}_{\infty,\omega}
class. To this end, we will define a cardinal
\mu(K)
by:

Definition 3.16
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation which is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal, and admits decomposition.

(1)

For
M\in K
and
a\in M
, we define
\mu(a,M)
to be the least cardinal
\mu
such that: for any
M_{b}\leq M
and any sequence
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
such that
M
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, there is a subsequence
S\subseteq\alpha
with
|S|<\mu
such that
a\in\bigoplus^{M}_{M_{b},j\in S}M_{j}
.

(2)

We define
\mu(M):=\text{sup}\{\mu(a,M):a\in M\}

(3)

We define
\mu(K):=\text{sup}\{\mu(M):M\in K\}
if it exists, or
\mu(K)=\infty
otherwise.

(4)

If
\mu(K)<\infty
, then we define
\mu_{r}(K)
to be the least regular cardinal
\geq\mu(K)
.

Remark
.

Strictly speaking,
\mu(K)
should be considered as
\mu^{\mathscr{A}}(K)
since the definition depends on
\mathscr{A}
and different notions of amalgamation might give rise to different values of
\mu(K)
. However, since in this paper we will always be considering a class
K
with a fixed notion of amalgamation
\mathscr{A}
, we have chosen to suppress the extra notation.

4.
An Independence Relation defined from
\mathscr{A}

In the elementary class of algebraically closed fields with characteristic 0, the forking relationship can be easily understood in terms of transcendental degrees:
\text{gtp}(\bar{a}/F_{1})
does not fork over
F_{0}
iff
\text{td}(\bar{a}/F_{1})=\text{td}(\bar{a}/F_{0})
. Since this is essentially a characterization of forking using the concept of bases, we would expect that a suitably well-behaved notion of amalgamation would also give rise to a forking-like independence relation. To that end, we define:

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
K
is an AEC,
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation in
K
. We define a notion of
\mathscr{A}
-independence, denoted by
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
, as follows: if
M\leq N
and
A,B\subseteq N
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
if there exists models
M_{1},M_{2}
with
M\leq M_{1},M_{2}\leq N
such that
A\subseteq M_{1},B\subseteq M_{2}
, and
M_{1},M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated inside
N
over
M
i.e. there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

In such a case, we say that the pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
is a
witness
to
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
.

Our goal here is to establish the conditions necessary for
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
to behave as forking for stationary types in a simple first order theories: To that end, we need to establish that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
has the defining properties of forking:

•

Invariance

•

Top monotonicity (i.e. forking does not depend on the ambient model)

•

Right monotonicity

•

Base monotonicity

•

Symmetry

•

Transitivity

•

Existence of nonforking extensions

•

Continuity

•

\kappa
-ary character for some cardinal
\kappa

•

Uniqueness of nonforking extensions

We will show that these properties hold through a series of propositions.

Proposition 4.2

(Top Monotonicity)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a notion of amalgamation.

(1)

If
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
and
N^{\prime}\geq N
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N^{\prime}}B

(2)

Suppose that
K
admits finite intersection and
\mathscr{A}
is regular, minimal. If
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
and
N^{\prime}\leq N
is such that
A,B,M\subseteq N^{\prime}
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N^{\prime}}B

Proof.

That (1) is true is straightforward from the definition of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
. For (2), let
(M_{1},M_{2})
witness that
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
; as
K
has FI and
M\leq M_{1},M_{2},N^{\prime}\leq N
, both
M_{1}\cap N^{\prime}
and
M_{2}\cap N^{\prime}
are models of
K
, and by regularity
M_{1}\cap N^{\prime},M_{2}\cap N^{\prime}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M
inside
N
. Since
K
admits finite intersection and
\mathscr{A}
is minimal, hence
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal by Lemma
2.11
, and so in particular
(M_{1}\cap N^{\prime})\oplus_{M}^{N}(M_{2}\cap N^{\prime})\leq N^{\prime}
. Hence
(M_{1}\cap N^{\prime},M_{2}\cap N^{\prime})
is a witness to
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N^{\prime}}B
.
∎

Some straightforward observations which follow from the definition of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
are:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a notion of amalgamation

(1)

(Existence) For any
M\leq N
and
A\subseteq N
,
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}M
.

(2)

(Symmetry)
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
implies
B\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}A
.

(3)

(Right Monotonicity) If
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B^{\prime}
.

(4)

(Right Normality)
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
iff
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}(B\cup M)

Proposition 4.4

(Base Monotonicity)
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular. If
M^{\prime}
is such that
M\leq M^{\prime}\subseteq B
and
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M^{\prime}}^{N}B
.

Proof.

Let
(M_{1},M_{2})
witness that
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
. In particular, this implies that there is some
N^{\prime}\leq N
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Since
M\leq M^{\prime}\leq M_{2}
(as
M^{\prime}\subseteq B\subseteq M_{2}
), by regularity there exists some
N^{\prime\prime}\leq N^{\prime}
with

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Since
A\subseteq M_{1}\leq N^{\prime\prime}
, hence
(N^{\prime\prime},M_{2})
is a witness to
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M^{\prime}}^{N}B
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is regular and
M_{0}\leq M_{1}
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
iff there is some
M_{2}\leq N
such that
M_{0}\leq M_{2}
,
A\subseteq M_{2}
and
M_{1},M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
.

Proof.

For the reverse direction, note that
(M_{2},M_{1})
is a witness to
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
. For the forward direction, let
(M_{2},M^{\prime})
witness that
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
, and so in particular
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq M^{\prime}
. Hence by regularity,
M_{1},M_{2}
are also
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
.
∎

Proposition 4.6

(Transitivity)
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersection and
\mathscr{A}
is regular, absolutely minimal. If
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq M_{2}\leq N
and
A\subseteq N
is such that
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
and
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{1}}^{N}M_{2}
, then
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}
.

Proof.

By the above lemma, there exists
M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}\leq N
such that
(M^{\prime},M_{1})
witnesses
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
and
(M^{\prime\prime},M_{2})
witnesses
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{1}}^{N}M_{2}
. Hence, there are also models
M^{\prime}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1},M^{\prime\prime}\oplus_{M_{1}}^{N}M_{2}\leq N
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}\oplus_{M_{1}}^{N}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Since
K
has FI and
M_{0}\leq M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}\leq N
, there is a model
M^{*}:=M^{\prime}\cap M^{\prime\prime}
, and in particular
M_{0}\leq M^{*},A\subseteq M^{*}
. So by regularity,
M^{*},M_{1}
are also
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{0}
inside
N
i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Note that since
M_{1},M^{*}\leq M^{\prime\prime}
,
M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{*}\leq M^{\prime\prime}
as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal. Therefore, again by regularity,
(M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{*}),M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated over
M_{1}
inside
N
, so there is some
M^{**}
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Combining the commutative squares on the left of the two diagrams, we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Applying regularity once more, hence
(M^{*},M_{2})
witness that
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 4.7

(Invariance)
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation, then
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is invariant under
K
-embeddings: if
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N}B
and
f:N\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
is a
K
-embedding, then
f(A)\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{f[M]}^{N^{\prime}}f(B)
.

Proof.

First, for the case where
f:N\cong N^{\prime}
is a
K
-isomorphism, the statement above holds due to the Invariance properties of
\mathscr{A}
. Then Proposition
4.2
shows that this is true for the general case where
f
is a
K
-embedding.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

If
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
and
\text{gtp}(\bar{a}/M_{1},N)=\text{gtp}(\bar{b}/M_{1},N^{\prime})
, then
\bar{b}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N^{\prime}}M_{1}
.

The above corollary shows that when
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is derived from
\mathscr{A}
, then in fact
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
can be extended to a form of nonforking notion for Galois types.

Notation 4.9
.

Let
p\in S(M_{1})
. We say that
p

does not fork
over
M_{0}
if
M_{0}\leq M_{1}
and there is some
\bar{a}
and a model
N\geq M_{1}
such that
(\bar{a},M_{1},N)
realize
p
, and
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
.

We say that
q\geq p
is a
nonforking extension
if
q
does not fork over
\text{dom }p

Corollary 4.10
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersection, and
\mathscr{A}
is regular, absolutely minimal. If
p
does not fork over
M
and
q
is a nonforking extension of
p
, then
q
does not fork over
M
.

Proposition 4.11

(Extension)
.

Let
p\in S(M_{0})
. If
M_{1}\geq M_{0}
, then there is
q\in S(M_{1})
such that
q\geq p
and
q
does not fork over
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
(\bar{a},M_{0},M_{2})
realize
p
, and let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{2},M_{1}
over
M_{0}
via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Then
f(\bar{a})\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{1}
(as witnessed by
(f[M_{2}],M_{1})
), and
\text{gtp}(f(\bar{a})/M_{0},N)=p
. Hence
\text{gtp}(f(\bar{a})/M_{1},N)
is the desired nonforking extension.
∎

Proposition 4.12

(Locality, version 1)
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal and admits decomposition. Assume further that
\mu(K)<\infty
. If
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is a strictly increasing continuous chain of models and
\text{cf}(\alpha)\geq\mu_{r}(K)
, then for every
p\in S(\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i})
a Galois type of length
<\mu(K)
, there is some
i<\alpha
such that
p
does not fork over
M_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
M_{b}:=M_{0},M:=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}
, and let us define a sequence of models
(M_{i}^{\prime})_{i<\alpha}
such that:

(1)

For each
i<\alpha
,
M_{b}\leq M_{i}^{\prime}\leq M_{i+1}
.

(2)

M_{0}^{\prime}=M_{1}

(3)

For each
i<\alpha
,
M_{i}^{\prime}
is such that
M_{i}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M}M_{i}^{\prime}=M_{i+1}
.

Note that
\mathscr{A}
admitting decomposition implies that such a sequence exists. Furthermore, by construction we have that
\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{M}M_{i}^{\prime}=M
(as witnessed by the resolution
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
).

Given
p\in S(M)
a Galois type of length
<\mu(K)
, let
(\bar{a},M,N)
realize
p
, and let
N^{*}\leq N
be such that
N=M\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}N^{*}
(again,
N^{*}
exists as
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition). Hence we also have that
N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
\{N^{*}\}\cup\{M_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
(by inclusion). Since
|\bar{a}|<\mu_{r}(K)\leq\text{cf}(\alpha)
, there is some
i_{0}<\alpha
such that

\bar{a}\in N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<i_{0}}^{N}M_{i}^{\prime}\bigg)=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i_{0}+1}

Let
N^{\prime}:=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i_{0}+1}
. Since
N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{*},M
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, by regularity we also have that
N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{\prime},M
over
M_{i_{0}+1}
. Diagrammatically,

\hbox to86.82pt{\vbox to47.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.41103pt\lower-23.92906pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Note then that
(N^{\prime},M)
is a witness to
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{i_{0}+1}}^{N}M
, and therefore
p
does not fork over
M_{i_{0}+1}
.
∎

In fact, a related formulation of the locality property can be shown to be true using the same proof:

Proposition 4.13

(Locality, version 2)
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal, admits decomposition and is such that
\mu(K)<\infty
. If
|M|>\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
and
p
is a Galois type over
M
of length
<\mu(K)
, then there is some
M^{*}\leq M
such that
|M^{*}|<\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)^{+}
and
p
does not fork over
M^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=|M|
, and take some
M_{b}\leq M
with
|M_{b}|=\text{LS}(K)
. As
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition and is absolutely minimal, by Lemma
3.15
there is a sequence
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
such that:

(1)

\alpha<\lambda^{+}

(2)

For each
i<\alpha
,
M_{b}\leq M_{i}\leq M
and
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)

(3)

M=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{M}M_{i}

Further, as
\mathscr{A}
is regular and continuous, by Theorem
3.14
we may assume that
\alpha=\lambda
. Letting
(\bar{a},M,N)
be a realization of
p
, as in the proof for the above proposition there exists some
N^{*}
such that
N=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M
. Now, as
|A|<\mu(K)<\infty
, there is some subset
S\subseteq\lambda
such that
|S|<\mu_{r}(K)
and
A\subseteq N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\big(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S}^{N}M_{i}\big)
. Letting
N^{\prime}=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\big(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S}^{N}M_{i}\big)
, hence (as in the above proof)
N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N^{\prime},M
over
\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S}^{N}M_{i}
by regularity. Therefore, letting
M^{*}=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S}^{N}M_{i}
, we have
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M^{*}}^{N}M
. Furthermore, as
|S|<\mu_{r}(K)
and each
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)
, by Lemma
2.12
,
|M^{*}|<\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)^{+}
as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersection, and
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal, admits decomposition, and is such that
\mu(K)<\infty
. If
M\in K
,
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is continuous resolution of
M
with
\text{cf}(\alpha)\geq\mu_{r}(K)
, and
p\in S(M)
is a type of length
<\mu(K)
such that each
p\upharpoonright M_{i}
does not fork over
M_{0}
, then
p
does not fork over
M_{0}
.

Proof.

By the previous proposition, there is some
i<\alpha
such that
p
does not fork over
M_{i}
. But
p\upharpoonright M_{i}
does not fork over
M_{0}
by assumption, and so by Proposition
4.6

p
does not fork over
M_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 4.15

(Uniqueness)
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersection,
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness and is regular. Then for any Galois type
p\in S(M)
and any
N\geq M
, there is a unique
q\in S(N)
such that
q
is a nonforking extension of
p
.

Proof.

Let
q_{1},q_{2}\in S(N)
be nonforking extensions of
p
, and let
(\bar{a},N,N_{1}),(\bar{b},N,N_{2})
be realizations of the types respectively. Since
q_{1}\upharpoonright M=q_{2}\upharpoonright M=p
and
K
has AP (since
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation), we may assume that there is a
K
-isomorphism
f:N_{1}\longrightarrow_{M}N_{2}
such that
f(\bar{a})=\bar{b}
. Now, as each
q_{i}
is a nonforking extension of
p
, there exists
M_{1}\leq N_{1}
such that
(M_{1},N)
is a witness to
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N_{1}}N
, and similarly
M_{2}\leq N_{2}
. Letting
M^{\prime\prime}=f[M_{1}]\cap M_{2}
, note then that
M\leq M^{\prime\prime},\bar{b}\in M^{\prime\prime}
. Hence, by regularity,
(M^{\prime\prime},N)
is also a witness for
\bar{b}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N_{2}}N
. Further, let
M^{\prime}\leq N_{1}
be such that
f[M^{\prime}]=M^{\prime\prime}
, and similarly
(M^{\prime},N)
is a witness for
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}^{N_{1}}N
. But as
f
is also an isomorphism between
M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
over
M
, by uniqueness of
\mathscr{A}
there is an isomorphism
g
satisfying the following commutative diagram (where all the unlabelled maps are inclusions):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\oplus_{M}^{N_{1}}M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\oplus_{M}^{N_{2}}M^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

In particular,
g(\bar{a})=f(\bar{a})=f(\bar{b})=g(\bar{b})
and
g[N]=N
, and hence
\text{gtp}(\bar{a},N,N_{1})=\text{gtp}(\bar{b},N,N_{2})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

Suppose
K
admits finite intersection,
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness and is regular. If
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is an increasing continuous chain, and
(p_{i})_{i<\alpha}
is an increasing chain of types (with each
p_{i}\in S(M_{i})
) such that each
p_{i+1}
is a nonforking extension of
p_{i}
, then there is
p\in S\big(\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}\big)
such that
p\upharpoonright M_{i}=M_{i}
and
p
does not fork over
M_{0}
.

Proof.

Denote
M_{\alpha}:=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{i}
. By Proposition
4.11
, let
p\in S(M_{\alpha})
be a nonforking extension of
p_{0}
. Note then that for each
i<\alpha
,
p\upharpoonright M_{i}
also does not fork over
M_{0}
, and hence is a nonforking extension of
p_{0}
. By the above proposition, hence
p\upharpoonright M_{i}=p_{i}
.
∎

Definition 4.17
.

We say that
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of
geometric amalgamation
if
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal, and admits decomposition with
\mu(K)<\infty
. We say that
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of
free amalgamation
if additionally
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness.

Conclusion 4.18
.

Suppose
K
is an AEC admitting finite intersections,
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation on
K
. Then the relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
satisfies:

•

Invariance (Proposition
4.7
)

•

Existence (Proposition
4.3
.
1
)

•

Symmetry (Proposition
4.3
.
2
)

•

Top monotonicity (Proposition
4.2
)

•

Right (and left) monotonicity (Proposition
4.3
.
3
and symmetry)

•

Base monotonicity (Proposition
4.4
)

•

Normality (Proposition
4.3
.
4
)

•

Transitivity (Proposition
4.6
)

•

Extension (Proposition
4.11
)

•

\mu(K)
-local character for types of length
<\mu(K)
(Proposition
4.12
and
4.13
)

•

Continuity (Proposition
4.14
)

•

Uniqueness of nonforking extensions (Proposition
4.15
)

This completes the propositions needed to prove that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
has the desired properties (under certain assumptions on
K
and
\mathscr{A}
). A nontrivial example of such an independence relation comes from the class of free groups:

Example 4.19
.

Let
K
be the class of free groups, with an ordering
\leq_{f}
such that
G\leq_{f}H
iff
G
is a free factor in
H
i.e. there is some set
Y
such that we can consider
H=F(Y)
(the free group with
Y
as the set of generators), and moreover there is some
X\subseteq Y
such that
G=F(X)
.

Note then that
K
is a weak AEC which admits finite intersections (see the Appendix for details), and taking
\mathscr{A}
to be the notion of free amalgamation gives us that
\mathscr{A}
is minimal (hence absolutely minimal), regular, continuous, admits decomposition, and has uniqueness. It is also clear that
\mu(K)=\aleph_{0}
. In this case,
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{F}^{G}\bar{b}
iff there is a free basis
X
of
G
(so
F(X)=G
) along with subsets
X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}\subseteq X
such that:

•

F_{0}=F(X_{0})

•

X_{1}\cap X_{2}=X_{0}

•

\bar{a}\in F(X_{1})
and
\bar{b}\in F(X_{2})

Moreover, the above lemmas show that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
for the class of free groups behaves as if for a superstable first order theory; this is not surprising since by defining superstability in terms of uniqueness of limit models, the uncountable categoricity of the class implies that it is indeed superstable as an AEC.

On the other hand, this example is notable for two reasons:

(1)

The free factors of a free group are
not
closed under infinite intersections (for an example, see
[
BCS77
]
), and in particular the class of free groups do not admit arbitrary intersection. This is in contrast to classes such as vector spaces and algebraically closed fields, where the pregeometry is used to define independence but implies that the class admits intersections.

(2)

The first order theory of free groups is known to be not superstable (see, for example,
[
Poi83
]
), whereas
(K,\leq_{f})
is indeed superstable as an AEC. Furthermore, since
G\leq_{f}H
imlies that
G
is an elementary substructure of
H
(see the Appendix), this implies that the free groups lies within the superstable part of the theory of free groups. This fact is, of course, trivial given that the free groups are uncountably categorical, but does show how different the class of free groups is from the class of elementarily free groups.

Before ending this section, let us demonstrate the known fact that the existence of a superstable-like independence notion implies that the class is tame:

Definition 4.20
.

Let
I
be a linear order. We say that
K
is
(<\lambda)
-tame for
I
-types
if for any model
M
and
p,q\in S^{I}(M)
,
p\neq q
iff there exists some
N\leq M
such that
|N|<\lambda
and
p\upharpoonright N\neq q\upharpoonright N
. We say that
K
is
\lambda
-tame if it is
(<\lambda^{+})
-tame.

Corollary 4.21
.

If
K
admits finite intersection and
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation, then
K
is
(\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K))^{+}
-tame for types of length
<\mu(K)
.

Proof.

Let
M\in K
with
|M|>\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
,
p,q\in S(M)
be types of length
l<\mu(K)
, and let
(\bar{a},M,N_{1}),(\bar{b},M,N_{2})
realize
p,q
respectively. By Proposition
4.13
, there is
M_{a}\leq M
such that
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{a}}^{N_{1}}M
and
|M_{a}|=\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
. Define
M_{b}\leq M
similarly, and (by the Löwenheim-Skolem property) let
M_{0}\leq M
be such that
M_{a},M_{b}\leq M_{0}
and
|M_{0}|=\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
. Then by Proposition
4.4
,
\bar{a}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N_{1}}M
and
\bar{b}\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}^{N_{2}}M
.
Now, if
p,q
are such that
p\upharpoonright M^{\prime}=q\upharpoonright M^{\prime}
for every
M^{\prime}\leq M
with
|M^{\prime}|\leq\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
, then in particular
p\upharpoonright M_{0}=q\upharpoonright M_{0}
. But
p
is a nonforking extension of
p\upharpoonright M_{0}
and similarly
q
, so by Proposition
4.15
,
p=q
.
∎

Remark
.

The statement of the the above Corollary begs comparison to a result of Boney that appears as Theorem 3.7 of
[
Vas17
]
, stating that a pseudouniversal AEC is
(\aleph_{0})
-tame.
1
1

1

The actual result is slightly stronger, but difficult to state here precisely due to small conflicts of notation.
Since pseudouniversality is a strengthening
2
2

2

To quote
[
Vas17
]
, the extra requirement is that “the isomorphism characterizing equality of Galois types is unique”.
of admitting intersections with
\mu(K)=\aleph_{0}
(when a suitable notion of amalgamation is defined), at first glance our result appears to be comparable. Besides the slightly different cardinal arithmetic, the main difference is that our result here relaxes the intersection requirement to only finite intersections, but at the expense of requiring
\mathscr{A}
to have uniqueness (which, as Section 6 explores, has strong implications regarding the structure of
K
and is not a trivial assumption).

5.
Categoricity Transfer Using Unique Amalgams

Up until this point, we have three primary examples of classes with a notion of free amalgamation which have guided our exploration:

•

The class of vector spaces over a fixed field with the subspace (equivalently, elementary submodel) ordering

•

The class of (torsion) divisible groups with the subgroup ordering

•

The class of free groups with the “free factor” ordering (see Example
4.19
)

The key characteristic shared, and indeed the driving intuition for this study, is that such classes have some notion of “basis” which generates each model. Now, in the case of the class of vector spaces, the eventual categoricity of the class can be derived from the fact that any bijection between two bases extends to an isomorphism between the spanned spaces. An analogous principle clearly holds also for the free groups, and the same argument can be applied more generally to the cases of strongly minimal first order theories and quasiminimal excellent classes with the countable closure property. On the other hand, this does
not
apply to the class of divisible groups, and the torsion divisible groups are not categorical in any cardinal whereas the class of free groups are uncountably categorical. In this sense, we will formalize the intuitive argument above to establish sufficient conditions for a categoricity transfer theorem.

One aspect of the argument above for vector spaces is that two superspaces
V,W
of
U
are isomorphic over
U
if
V/U,W/U
have the same dimension. Although there is no notion of dimensionality within the current context, we note that the dimension of a vector space only differs from its cardinality for spaces of small dimension. This allows us to formalize the notion of two extensions being “isomorphic” when they are of sufficiently large cardinality:

Definition 5.1
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
.

(1)

We define
\theta(K)=\mu(K)+\text{LS}(K)

(2)

Given
M_{b}\leq M_{t}
and
\alpha
an ordinal, for a model
N\geq M_{b}
we write “
N\cong M_{t}^{\lambda}/M_{b}
” to indicate that
N=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N}M_{i}
, where each
M_{i}\cong_{M_{b}}M_{t}
.

(3)

We define an equivalence relation
\backsim
on pairs of models of
K
by: given
M_{1}\leq N_{1}
and
M_{2}\leq N_{2}
,
(N_{1},M_{1})\backsim(N_{2},M_{2})
iff there is a
K
-isomorphism
f:N_{1}^{\theta(K)}/M_{1}\cong N_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{2}
with
f[M_{1}]=M_{2}
.

Remark
.

Note that the above definition does not construct
M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
as a particular model, but if
N_{1},N_{2}
are such that both
N_{1}\cong M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
and
N_{2}\cong M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
, then in fact
N_{1}\cong_{M_{b}}N_{2}
by uniqueness of
\mathscr{A}
, and hence we may consider
M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
as a particular choice of representative inside
K
. In this sense, for any ordinal
0<\beta<\alpha
we may consider
M_{b}\leq M_{t}\leq M_{t}^{\beta}/M_{b}\leq M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
. In this sense, we extend the notation by defining
M_{t}^{0}/M_{b}=M_{b}

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation. If
(N_{1},M)\backsim(N_{2},M)
, then for any
\lambda\geq\theta(K)
, there is a
K
-isomorphism
f:N_{1}^{\lambda}/M\cong_{M}N_{2}^{\lambda}/M
.

Proof.

Decompose
\lambda=\bigsqcup_{j<\lambda}S_{j}
such that each
|S_{j}|=\theta(K)
. Defining models
N_{1}^{*},N_{1}^{i},N_{2}^{*},N_{2}^{i}
such that
N_{l}^{*}=\bigoplus_{M,i<\lambda}^{N_{l}^{*}}N_{l}^{i}
for
l=1,2
, note then that for each
j,j^{\prime}<\lambda
,

\bigoplus_{M,i\in S_{j}}^{N_{1}^{*}}N_{1}^{i}\cong N_{1}^{\theta(K)}/M\cong N_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M\cong\bigoplus_{M,i\in S_{j^{\prime}}}^{N_{2}^{*}}N_{2}^{i}

So let us define
N_{l}^{S_{j}}=\bigoplus_{M,i\in S_{j}}^{N_{l}^{*}}N_{1}^{i}
. Then, by applying Theorem
3.13
, we get that
N_{l}^{*}=\bigoplus_{M,j<\lambda}^{N_{l}^{*}}N_{l}^{S_{j}}
. Hence, as
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness, we get that
N_{1}^{*},N_{2}^{*}
are isomorphic over
M
.
∎

Definition 5.3
.

Given
K
an AEC, we say that
M\in K
is a
prime and minimal model
of
K
if:

(1)

For every
N\in K
, there is a
K
-embedding
\iota_{N}:M\longrightarrow N
; and

(2)

For every
K
-embedding
f:N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{2}
,
f\circ\iota_{N_{1}}=\iota_{N_{2}}

If
K
has a prime and minimal model, we fix such a model and denote it by
0_{K}
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
, and
0_{K}
is a prime and minimal model. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical in some
\lambda\geq\theta(K)
, then for any
M_{1},M_{2}
in
K
with
|M_{1}|=|M_{2}|=\text{LS}(K)
,
(M_{1},0_{K})\backsim(M_{2},0_{K})
.

Proof.

Given
M_{1},M_{2}
and
\lambda
as above, note that by Lemma
3.15
,
|M_{1}^{\lambda}/0_{K}|=|M_{2}^{\lambda}/0_{K}|=\lambda
. Hence, by
\lambda
-categoricity, there is some
K
-isomorphism
f:M_{1}^{\lambda}/0_{K}\cong M_{2}^{\lambda}/0_{K}
, and moreover
f[0_{K}]=0_{K}
as
0_{K}
is prime and minimal. Thus, WLOG we may assume that
N=M_{1}^{\lambda}/0_{K}=M_{2}^{\lambda}/0_{K}
, and in fact that there exists sequence
(M_{1}^{i})_{i<\lambda},(M_{2}^{i})_{i<\lambda}
such that:

(1)

For each
i<\lambda
,
M_{1}^{i}
is isomorphic to
M_{1}
and
M_{2}^{i}
is isomorphic to
M_{2}
(over
0_{K}
).

(2)

Each
0_{K}\leq M_{1}^{i},M_{2}^{i}\leq N
; and

(3)

N=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<\lambda}^{N}M_{1}^{i}=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<\lambda}^{N}M_{2}^{i}

We will construct two sequences of sets
(S_{j})_{j<\omega},(T_{j})_{j<\omega}
, satisfying:

(1)

Each
S_{j}\subseteq\lambda
with
|S_{j}|=\theta(K)
, and similarly for
T_{j}

(2)

S_{0}=T_{0}=\theta(K)

(3)

S_{j}\subseteq S_{j+1}
and
T_{j}\subseteq T_{j+1}

(4)

For each
j<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{j}}^{N}M_{2}^{i}\leq\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{j+1}}^{N}M_{1}^{i}
; and

(5)

For each
j<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{j}}^{N}M_{1}^{i}\leq\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{j+1}}^{N}M_{2}^{i}

Let us first show that such a construction is sufficient: defining
S:=\bigcup_{j<\omega}S_{j}
and
T:=\bigcup_{j<\omega}T_{j}
, note that as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal,

\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S}^{N}M_{1}^{i}=\bigcup_{j<\omega}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{j}}^{N}M_{1}^{i}\Bigg)

The same statement holds for
T
and
M_{2}^{i}
. Hence, by (3) and (4) of the construction above, we have that
\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S}^{N}M_{1}^{i}=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T}^{N}M_{2}^{i}
. But since
|S|=|T|=\theta(K)
, hence we can take
\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S}M_{1}^{i}\cong M_{1}^{\theta(K)}/0_{K}
, and therefore
(M_{1},0_{K})\backsim(M_{2},0_{K})
.

Let us complete the proof by constructing
S_{j},T_{j}
. Given
S_{j},T_{j}
already defined, consider
M^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{j}}^{N}M_{2}^{i}
: by Lemma
2.12
,
|M^{\prime}|=\text{LS}(K)+|T_{j}|=\theta(K)
, and hence there is
S_{j+1}\subseteq\lambda
such that
|S_{j+1}|=\theta(K)+\mu_{r}(K)=\theta(K)
and
M^{\prime}\subseteq\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{j+1}}^{N}M_{1}^{i}
. Similarly we can define
T_{j+1}
, and this completes the proof.
∎

Note that the conclusion of the above theorem holds for the classes of vector spaces and free groups, but not for divisible groups: for example, letting
0_{G}
denote the trivial group, it is clear that if
p\neq q
are primes, then
(\mathbb{Z}(p^{\infty}),0_{G}),(\mathbb{Z}(q^{\infty}),0_{G})
are not
\backsim
equivalent.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
. Given models
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}
, if
(M_{1},M_{0})\backsim(M_{2},M_{0})
, then for any ordinal
\beta
,

(M_{1}^{\beta}/M_{0})\oplus_{M_{0}}(M_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{0})\cong_{M_{0}}M_{1}^{|\beta|+\theta(K)}/M_{0}\cong_{M_{0}}M_{2}^{|\beta|+\theta(K)}/M_{0}

Proof.

As
(M_{1},M_{0})\backsim(M_{2},M_{0})
,
M_{1}^{\theta(K)}/M_{0}\cong_{M_{0}}M_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{0}
, and hence

(M_{1}^{\beta}/M_{0})\oplus_{M_{0}}(M_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{0})\cong_{M_{0}}M_{1}^{\beta+\theta(K)}/M_{0}

Furthermore, by Theorem
3.14
and Lemma
5.2
, we have that

M_{1}^{\beta+\theta(K)}/M_{0}\cong_{M_{0}}M_{1}^{|\beta|+\theta(K)}/M_{0}\cong_{M_{0}}M_{2}^{|\beta|+\theta(K)}/M_{0}

∎

Theorem 5.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
, and
K
has a prime and minimal model. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical in some
\lambda\geq\theta(K)
, then
K
is
\kappa
-categorical in every cardinal
\kappa\geq\theta(K)+(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}
.

Proof.

By the previous theorem, for any
M_{1},M_{2}\in K_{\text{LS}(K)}
,
(M_{1},0_{K})\backsim(M_{2},0_{K})
. Hence by Lemma
5.2
, it suffices to show that if
M\in K
and
|M|=\kappa\geq\theta(K)+(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}
, then
M\cong M^{\prime\kappa}/0_{K}
for some
M^{\prime}\in K_{\text{LS}(K)}
.

So given
M\in K
and
|M|=\kappa
, by Lemma
3.15
we can decompose
M=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<\kappa}^{M}M_{i}
such that each
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)
. Letting
\Gamma:=\{M_{i}/\cong:i<\kappa\}
(where
\cong
is the equivalence relation of
K
-isomorphism), note that since
|\Gamma|\leq 2^{\text{LS}(K)}
, there is some
P\in\Gamma
which is realized
\geq\theta(K)+(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}
times in the sequence
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
. For each
Q\in\Gamma
, let us also fix some
M_{Q}\in\{M_{i}:i<\kappa\}
such that
M_{Q}\vDash Q
. Note that by the previous theorem, for any
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\Gamma
,
(M_{Q_{1}},0_{K})\backsim(M_{Q_{2}},0_{K})
.

Defining
S:=\{i\in\kappa:M_{i}\vDash P\}
, we can decompose
S
as a disjoint union
S=\bigsqcup_{Q\in\Gamma}S_{Q}
which is indexed by
\Gamma
and such that each
|S_{Q}|\geq\theta(K)+(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}
and is a regular cardinal (possibly except for
S_{P}
). Now, for each
Q\in\Gamma
, we have that
\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{Q}}^{M}M_{i}\cong M_{P}^{|S_{Q}|}/0_{K}
as each
i\in S_{Q}\subseteq S
. Defining
N_{S_{Q}}=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S_{Q}}^{M}M_{i}
, note that as
|S_{Q}|\geq\theta(K)
and
(M_{P},0_{K})\backsim(M_{Q},0_{K})
, by Theorem
3.13
there is a sequence
(N_{Q}^{i})_{i<|S_{Q}|}
such that
N_{S_{Q}}=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<|S_{Q}|}^{M}N_{Q}^{i}
and such that each
N_{Q}^{i}\vDash Q
.

Now, for each
Q\in\Gamma
such that
Q\neq P
, let
T_{Q}:=\{i\in\kappa:M_{i}\vDash Q\}
, and define
N^{*}_{Q}:=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{Q}}^{M}M_{i}
. By Theorems
3.13
and
3.14
, each
N_{S_{Q}},N^{*}_{Q}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated inside
M
over
0_{K}
, and so we have that

N_{S_{Q}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}N^{*}_{Q}=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<|S_{Q}|}^{M}N_{Q}^{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{Q}}^{M}M_{i}\Bigg)

In other words,
N_{S_{Q}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}N^{*}_{Q}\cong M_{Q}^{|S_{Q}|+|T_{Q}|}/0_{K}
by Lemma
5.2
. In particular, as
(M_{Q},0_{K})\backsim(M_{P},0_{K})
, we also have that
N_{S_{Q}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}N^{*}_{Q}\cong M_{P}^{|S_{Q}|+|T_{Q}|}/0_{K}
.

This implies that

\displaystyle M=\bigoplus_{0_{K},i<\kappa}^{M}M_{i}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in S}^{M}M_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},Q\neq P}^{M}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},i\in T_{Q}}^{M}M_{i}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=N_{S_{P}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{0_{K},Q\neq P}^{M}N_{S_{Q}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}N^{*}_{Q}\Bigg)

Since
N_{S_{P}}\cong M_{P}^{|S_{P}|}/0_{K}
and
N_{S_{Q}}\oplus_{0_{K}}^{M}N^{*}_{Q}\cong M_{P}^{|S_{Q}|+|T_{Q}|}/0_{K}
, thus we get that
M\cong M_{P}^{\kappa}/0_{K}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Note that in the above argument, the fact that
\lambda>2^{\text{LS}(K)}
was used to ensure that
|\Gamma|<\lambda
, and hence some
P\in\Gamma
is realized by many
M_{i}
’s. In particular, since each
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)
, in fact we can bound
|\Gamma|\leq I(K,\text{LS}(K))
, where
I(K,\theta)
is the number of non-isomorphic models in
K_{\theta}
. This gives the following strengthening:

Theorem 5.7
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation in
K
, and
K
has a prime and minimal model. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical in some
\lambda\geq\theta(K)
, then
K
is
\kappa
-categorical in every cardinal
\kappa\geq\theta(K)+I(K,\text{LS}(K))^{+}
.

This concludes our study of categoricity transfer in the case where there is a prime and minimal model, which for most algebraic examples is the trivial object inside the class. On the other hand, this is a very strong assumption from a model-theoretic point of view; for example, intuitively the class of saturated algebraically closed fields (equivalently, the algebreically closed fields of infinite transcendental degree) should also allow the same argument for categoricity transfer, but the class lacks a prime and minimal model. In order to modify the above argument to work in this case, we need to strengthen the notion of amalgmation with an additional property:

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a notion of amalgamation that is regular and absolutely minimal. We say that
\mathscr{A}
is
3-monotonic
if the following condition is satisfied:
Given models
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}\leq N
such that

(1)

M_{1},M_{2}
are
\mathscr{A}
-subamalgamated inside
N
over
M_{0}
; and

(2)

N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{3},M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}
over
M_{0}
via inclusion

Then
N
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{3},M_{2}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{3}
over
M_{3}
.

Diagrammatically, if the following commutative squares are
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Then we also have the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

In particular, these models also form the following commutative diagram (simplifying
M_{ij}:=M_{i}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N}M_{j}
and where all the arrows are inclusion maps), where each face of the cube is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{13}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{23}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{12}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal and 3-monotonic. If
M=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{M}M_{i}
and
N=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M
, then
N=\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})
.

Proof.

Let
(M^{\prime}_{i})_{i<s(\alpha)}
be a continuous resolution of
M
