witnessing that
M
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\alpha}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion. As
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, we have that each
M^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N}M_{i}
. We will prove the statement by induction on
\alpha
:

(1)

When
\alpha=1
, the statement is trivially true.

(2)

If the
\delta
is a limit ordinal and the statement is true for all
\alpha<\delta
, then for each
\alpha<\delta
, we have

N^{\prime}_{\alpha}:=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{\alpha}=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N^{\prime}_{\alpha}}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N^{\prime}_{\alpha}}M_{i}\Bigg)=\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N^{\prime}_{\alpha}}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N^{\prime}_{\alpha}}M_{i})

Note that as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, we can replace all the superscript
N^{\prime}_{\alpha}
by
N
. As a result, we thus have:

(a)

N^{\prime}_{0}=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{0}=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{b}=N^{*}

(b)

N^{\prime}_{1}=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{1}

(c)

For
1<\alpha<\delta
,
N^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})

Hence, letting
N^{\prime}_{\delta}:=\bigcup_{i<\delta}N^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, the sequence
(N^{\prime}_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\delta}
is a witness to

N^{\prime}_{\delta}=\bigoplus_{N^{*},\alpha<\delta}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})

But as
N=N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\big(\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}M^{\prime}_{\alpha})
, that
\mathscr{A}
is continuous and absolutely minimal implies that

N=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\delta}N^{\prime}_{\alpha}=N^{\prime}_{\delta}

This completes the proof for the limit step.

(3)

If the inductive hypothesis is true for
\alpha
, then we have

N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)=\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})

Since
\mathscr{A}
is 3-monotonic, we therefore get the following diagram where each face of the cube is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{N}M_{i}}

In particular, the top face thus guarantees that

N=(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{\alpha})\oplus_{N^{*}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})\Bigg)=\bigoplus_{N^{*},i<\alpha+1}^{N}(N^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M_{i})

This completes the successor step of the proof.

∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation and is 3-monotonic. For any
M_{t}\geq M_{b}
and ordinals
0<\beta<\alpha
, let
N_{1}=M_{t}^{\beta}/M_{b}
and
N_{2}=M_{t}^{\beta+1}/M_{b}
. Then
M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}\cong N_{2}^{\alpha-\beta}/N_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
M=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha}^{M}M^{\prime}_{i}
where each
M^{\prime}_{i}\cong_{M_{b}}M_{t}
, and hence
M\cong M_{t}^{\alpha}/M_{b}
. Defining
M^{*}=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\beta}^{M}M^{\prime}_{i}
, note then that
M^{*}\cong M_{t}^{\beta}/M_{b}\cong_{M_{b}}N_{1}
. Moreover, therefore we have that for each
i
such that
\beta\leq i<\alpha
,
M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M}M^{\prime}_{i}\cong M_{t}^{\beta+1}/M_{b}\cong_{M_{b}}N_{2}
. Hence by the above lemma, we also have that

M=M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\alpha-\beta}^{M}M^{\prime}_{\beta+i}\Bigg)=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i<\alpha-\beta}^{M}(M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M}M^{\prime}_{\beta+i})\cong N_{2}^{\alpha-\beta}/N_{1}

∎

Theorem 5.11
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation and is 3-monotonic. Given
M_{1}\lneq M_{2},N_{1}\lneq N_{2}
models of cardinality
\text{LS}(K)
, define
M_{b}=M_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{1},M_{t}=M_{2}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{1}
and
N_{b},N_{t}
likewise. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical for some
\lambda>\theta(K)
, then
(M_{t},M_{b})\backsim(N_{t},N_{b})
. In particular,
M_{b}\cong N_{b}
.

Proof.

As before, note that
|N_{2}^{\lambda}/N_{1}|=|M_{2}^{\lambda}/M_{1}|=\lambda
, and hence we can consider
M_{2}^{\lambda}/M_{1}\cong N_{2}^{\lambda}/N_{1}
by
\lambda
-categoricity. In other words, there is a model
N\in K_{\lambda}
and models
(M^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\lambda},(N^{\prime}_{i})_{i<\lambda}
such that:

(1)

For each
i<\lambda
,
M_{1}\leq M^{\prime}_{i}\leq N
and
M^{\prime}_{i}\cong_{M_{1}}M_{2}

(2)

For each
i<\lambda
,
N_{1}\leq N^{\prime}_{i}\leq N
and
N^{\prime}_{i}\cong_{N_{1}}N_{2}

(3)

N=\bigoplus_{M_{1},i<\lambda}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{N_{1},i<\lambda}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}

First, we will construct sequences of sets
(S_{j})_{j<\omega},(T_{j})_{j<\omega}
satisfying:

(1)

Each
S_{j},T_{j}\subseteq\lambda
, and each
|S_{j}|,|T_{j}|=\theta(K)

(2)

(S_{j})_{j<\omega},(T_{j})_{j<\omega}
are increasing sequences of sets

(3)

For each
j<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S_{j}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}\leq\bigoplus_{N_{1},i\in T_{j+1}}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}

(4)

For each
j<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{N_{1},i\in T_{j}}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}\leq\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S_{j+1}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}

We will construct these sets by induction:

•

Since
|N_{1}|=\text{LS}(K)
, there is
S_{0}\subseteq\lambda
such that
|S_{0}|=\theta(K)
and
N_{1}\leq\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S_{0}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}
. Similarly we can define
T_{0}
such that
M_{1}\leq\bigoplus_{N_{1},i\in T_{0}}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}
.

•

If
T_{j}
is defined and
|T_{j}|=\theta(K)
, then
\bigoplus_{N_{1}.i\in T_{j}}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}
is of cardinality
\mu(K)+\text{LS}(K)
, and hence there is
S_{j+1}\subseteq\lambda
such that
|S_{j+1}|=\theta(K)
and satisfying (3). Similarly we can define
T_{j+1}
such that (4) is satisfied.

Letting
S=\bigcup_{j<\omega}S_{j}
and
T=\bigcup_{j<\omega}
, note then that
|S|=|T|=\theta(K)
, and therefore we have

M_{b}=M_{2}^{\theta(K)}/M_{1}\cong\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{N_{1},i\in T}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}\cong N_{2}^{\theta(K)}/N_{1}=N_{b}

Note that by Theorem
3.13
, we also have that

N=\bigoplus_{M_{1},i<\lambda}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M_{1}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\notin S}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}\Bigg)

So, letting
M^{*}=\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}
, we have by Lemma
5.9
that

N=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\notin S}^{N}(M^{*}\oplus_{M_{1}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i})

Furthermore, since
\bigoplus_{M_{1},i\in S}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{N_{1},i\in T}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i}
by construction of
S,T
, we also have that

N=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\notin T}^{N}(M^{*}\oplus_{M_{2}}^{N}N^{\prime}_{i})

Let us define
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}=M^{*}\oplus_{M_{1}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\notin S
, and note that (by Lemma
2.12
) we have
|M^{\prime\prime}_{i}|=|M^{*}|+|M^{\prime}_{i}|=\theta(K)
. Also, by definition we have that
M^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cong M_{2}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{1}=M_{t}
. Similarly defining
N^{\prime\prime}_{i}
for
i\notin T
, we thus have

N=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\notin S}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\notin T}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}

Since
\lambda>\theta(K)=|S|=|T|
, by re-indexing the sequences we may consider

N=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i<\lambda}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i<\lambda}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}

Now, let us define new sequences of sets
(U_{k})_{k<\omega},(V_{k})_{k<\omega}
such that

(1)

For each
k<\omega
,
U_{k},V_{k}\subseteq\lambda
and
|U_{k}|=|V_{k}|=\theta(K)

(2)

(U_{k})_{k<\omega},(V_{k})_{k<\omega}
are increasing sequences of sets

(3)

S_{0}=T_{0}=\theta(K)

(4)

For each
k<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in T_{k}}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}\leq\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{k+1}}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i}

(5)

For each
k<\omega
,
\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{k}}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i}\leq\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in T_{k+1}}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}

The construction is the same as in Theorem
5.4
and above, using the fact that since each
|U_{k}|=|V_{k}|=\theta(K)
,
\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{k}}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i},\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in T_{k}}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are also of cardinality
\theta(K)
. In particular, if
U=\bigcup_{k<\omega}U_{k}
and
V=\bigcup_{k<\omega}V_{k}
, then we again have that

M_{t}^{\theta}(K)/M_{b}\cong\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in U}^{N}M^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in V}^{N}N^{\prime\prime}_{i}\cong N_{t}^{\theta}(K)/N_{b}

This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 5.12
.

Let
K
be an AEC. We say that
K
has
common small models
if for any models
N_{1},N_{2}\in K_{>\text{LS}(K)}
, there is
M_{1},M_{2}\in K_{\text{LS}(K)}
such that
M_{1}\leq N_{1},M_{2}\leq N_{2}
and
M_{1}\cong M_{2}
.

Remark
.

(1)

If
K
is
\text{LS}(K)
-categorical, then
K
has common small models.

(2)

If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical, then
K_{\geq\lambda}
has common small models

Theorem 5.13
.

Suppose
K
has common small models, and
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation and is 3-monotonic. If
K
is
\lambda
-categorical for some
\lambda>\theta(K)
, then
K
is
\kappa
-categorical for any
\kappa>\theta(K)+2^{\text{LS}(K)}
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem using a variation of the proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Claim
.

Let
N\in K
with
\kappa:=|N|>\theta(K)+2^{\text{LS}(K)}
. Then for any
M_{b}\leq N
with
|M_{b}|=\text{LS}(K)
, there is
M_{t}
such that
M_{b}\leq M_{t}\leq N
,
|M_{t}|=\text{LS}(K)
, and
N\cong N_{t}^{\kappa}/N_{b}
, where
N_{b}\cong M_{t}^{\theta(K)}/M_{b}
and
N_{t}\cong M_{t}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{b}
.

By Lemma
3.15
, we can decompose
N=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\kappa}^{N}M_{i}
where each
|M_{i}|=\text{LS}(K)
. Letting
\Gamma:=\{M_{i}/\cong_{M_{b}}:i<\kappa\}
, for each
P\in\Gamma
let
S_{P}:=\{i\in\kappa:M_{i}\vDash P\}
, and hence in particular
\kappa=\bigsqcup_{P\in\Gamma}S_{P}
. Note that
|\Gamma|\leq 2^{\text{LS}(K)}+\theta(K)<\kappa
as
|M_{b}|=\text{LS}(K)
, and hence there is some
Q\in\Gamma
such that
|S_{Q}|>2^{\text{LS}(K)}+\theta(K)
. Additionally, for each
P\in\Gamma
, fix a
M_{P}\vDash P
.

Let us further decompose
S_{Q}=T^{*}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{P\in\Gamma}T_{P}
such that
|T^{*}|=\theta(K)
, and whenever
P\neq Q
,
|T_{P}|>\theta(K)
and is regular. Thus by Theorem
3.13
we have that

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i<\kappa}^{N}M_{i}=\bigoplus_{M_{b},P\in\Gamma}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S_{P}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in T^{*}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in T_{Q}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\bigoplus_{M_{b},P\neq Q}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S_{P}\sqcup T_{P}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)

Letting
M^{*}=\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in T^{*}}^{N}M_{i}
, note that as
T^{*}\subseteq S_{Q}
,
M_{i}\vDash Q
for each
i\in T^{*}
, and so
M^{*}\cong M_{Q}^{|T^{*}|}/M_{b}=M_{Q}^{\theta(K)}/M_{b}
. Now, as
\mathscr{A}
is 3-monotonic, by Lemma
5.9
, we have that

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in T^{*}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in T_{Q}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}\bigoplus_{M_{b},P\neq Q}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M_{b},i\in S_{P}\sqcup T_{P}}^{N}M_{i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in T_{Q}}^{N}(M_{i}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{*})\Bigg)\oplus_{M^{*}}^{N}\bigoplus_{M^{*},P\neq Q}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{P}\sqcup T_{p}}^{N}(M_{i}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{*})\Bigg)

So for
i\notin T^{*}
, let
M^{\prime}_{i}:=M_{i}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{*}
. In particular, for any
P\in\Gamma
and
i\in T_{P}\subseteq S_{Q}
,
M^{\prime}_{i}\cong M_{Q}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{b}
. Furthermore, by Theorem
5.11
, for any
P\in\Gamma
,
M^{*}\cong M_{Q}^{\theta(K)}/M_{b}\cong M_{P}^{\theta(K)}/M_{b}
, and so in fact for any
P\neq Q
and
i\in S_{P}
,
M^{\prime}_{i}=M_{i}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{N}M^{*}\cong M_{P}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{b}
. Letting
N_{P}:=M_{P}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{b}
, hence by Theorem
5.11
, for any
P\in\Gamma
,
(N_{P},M^{*})\backsim(N_{Q},M^{*})
. So by Lemma
5.2
, since for any
P\neq Q
, as
|T_{P}|>\theta(K)
, we have

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{P}\sqcup T_{p}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in S_{P}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}\Bigg)\oplus_{M^{*}}^{N}\Bigg(\bigoplus_{M^{*},i\in T_{P}}^{N}M^{\prime}_{i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\cong(N_{P}^{|S_{P}|}/M^{*})\oplus_{M^{*}}(N_{Q}^{|T_{P}|}/M^{*})

\displaystyle\cong N_{Q}^{|S_{P}|+|T_{P}|}/M^{*}

Substituting this back, we get that

N\cong N_{Q}^{|\kappa-T^{*}|}/M^{*}=N_{Q}^{\kappa}/M^{*}

This proves the claim.

So given
M,N\in K_{\kappa}
with
\kappa>\theta(K)+2^{\text{LS}(K)}
, since
K
has common small models, let
M_{0}\leq M,N_{0}\leq N
such that
M_{0}\cong N_{0}
. By the above claim, there are models
M_{1},M_{b},M_{t},N_{1},N_{b},N_{t}
such that:

(1)

M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq M
and
N_{0}\leq N_{1}\leq N

(2)

M_{b}\cong M_{1}^{\theta(K)}/M_{0}
and
N_{b}\cong N_{1}^{\theta(K)}/N_{0}

(3)

M_{t}\cong M_{1}^{\theta(K)+1}/M_{0}
and
N_{t}\cong N_{1}^{\theta(K)+1}/N_{0}

(4)

M\cong M_{t}^{\kappa}/M_{b}
and
N\cong N_{t}^{\kappa}/N_{b}

Since
K
is
\lambda
-categorical for some
\lambda>\theta(K)
, by Theorem
5.11

(M_{t},M_{b})\backsim(N_{t},N_{b})
. Hence by Lemma
5.2
,
M\cong N
.
∎

Before ending this section, let us compare our result with other results of categoricity transfer which are relevant to our case:

Fact 5.14

(
[
GV06
]
, Theorem 6.3))
.

Suppose
K
is
\text{LS}(K)
-tame with the amalgamation property, joint embedding property, and arbitrary large models. If
K
is categorical in
\text{LS}(K)
and
\text{LS}(K)^{+}
, then
K
is categorical in all
\lambda\geq\text{LS}(K)

Fact 5.15

(
[
Vas18a
]
, Corollary 10.9)
.

Suppose
K
is
\text{LS}(K)
-tame, has arbitrary large models, and has primes. If
K
is categorical in some
\lambda>\text{LS}(K)
, then
K
is categorical in all
\lambda^{\prime}>\min(\lambda,\beth_{(2^{\text{LS}(K)})^{+}})

Fact 5.16

(
[
SV18
]
, Theorem 14.2)
.

Let
K
be an excellent AEC that is categorical in some
\mu>\text{LS}(K)
.

(1)

There is some
\chi<h(\text{LS}(K))
such that
K
is categorical in all
\mu^{\prime}\geq\min(\mu,\chi)
.
3
3

3

Recall that
h(\kappa):=\beth_{(2^{\kappa})^{+}}
.

(2)

If
K
is also categorical in
\text{LS}(K)
, then
K
is categorical in all
\mu^{\prime}>\text{LS}(K)
.

We note that classes with a notion of free amalgamation are
\mu_{r}(K)+\text{LS}(K)
-tame (see Lemma
4.21
), and hence Fact
5.14
is relevant here. On the other hand, many of the algebraic examples we have seen above are not
\text{LS}(K)
-categorical, but we manage to prove categoricity transfer using the additional assumption of a notion of free amalgamation.

With regards to Fact
5.15
, we recall from
[
Vas18a
]
that a class which admits (arbitrary) intersections over sets of the form
M\cup\{a\}
does have primes, and so in particular the result applies to AECs which admit intersection. Now, if the closure operator additionally satisfies the exchange principle (or if a suitable notion of “independent sets” can be otherwise defined), then it admits a 3-monotonic notion of geometric amalgamation (see also section 7 below). However, this still does not guarantee that the notion of amalgamation has uniqueness, and in this sense the extra assumptions of the exchange principle and uniqueness significantly brings down the cardinality threshold in proving categoricity transfer. On the other hand, the present result is applicable even to classes which do
not
have primes: for example, the class of free groups with free factor ordering.

Finally, regarding Fact
5.16
, there are two main points of comparison:

(1)

The relationship between
K
being excellent and
K
admitting a notion of free amalgamation is far from clear. Unlike the previous case, the greatest difference here is not regarding uniqueness but rather a sense of dimensionality:

•

For
\mathbb{I}
to be an excellent multidimensional independence relation, it must have
n
-existence and
n
-uniqueness for amalgamation diagrams of all finite dimensions.

•

For
\mathscr{A}
to be a notion of free amalgamation, it must admit decomposition and have bounded locality i.e.
\mu(K)<\infty
.

Using first order model theory as an analogy, the proof of Theorem
5.6
and
5.13
shows that free amalgamation along with categoricity in a sufficiently large cardinal implies that the class is essentially “unidimensional”, which implies that the class trivially has the NDOP (negation of the Dimensional Order Property). In contrast, the analysis of multidimensional amalgamation in excellence is a natural extension of analysing theories which have the NDOP but are not necessarily as simple as begin unidimensional. On the other hand, our formulation in terms of free amalgamation has also allowed us to prove the anti-structural theorems in the negative case (see Section 6 below), whereas a full main gap theorem from a multidimensional approach has yet to be reached.

(2)

The other point of comparison is of course the cardinal bounds present; we believe that this is due much more to the machinery used, and is a reflection of the different level of generality given in the first point.

6.
Amalgamation without Uniqueness, and having many Extensions

In the previous section, we proved arguably the strongest structural theorem which we could expect for classes with very “nice” notions of amalgamation. In particular, uniqueness of the notion of amalgamation was necessary to define the model
M_{t}^{\lambda}/M_{b}
, which was central to the argument above. On the other hand, having unique amalgams appears a priori to be a very strong assumption, and hence merits an investigation into when uniqueness can be derived.

The driving intuition here is that if a triple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
has two
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams which cannot be embedded into each other (w.r.t. to the triple), then by taking
\lambda
-many copies of
M_{1}
over
M_{0}
, we can construct
2^{\lambda}
-many models which cannot be embedded into each other. However, before we can formalize this argument, we need an additional property to hold for
\mathscr{A}
:

Definition 6.1
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of amalgamation and is regular. We say that
\mathscr{A}
has
weak 3-existence
if: given
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
, if
M_{ij}
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{i},M_{j}
over
M_{0}
, then there is a model
N
which is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{3},M_{12}
over
M_{0}
and such that there are
K
-embeddings
f_{1},f_{2}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{13}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{23}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{12}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

Remark
.

The “weak” in “weak 3-existence” indicates that in the above diagram, the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{12}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

is not necessarily an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam. Note that every other face of the cube is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam either by assumption or because
\mathscr{A}
is regular. Furthermore, if
\mathscr{A}
is 3-monotonic, then the above commutative square is also necessarily an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam.

Lemma 6.2
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is regular and has uniqueness, then
\mathscr{A}
has weak 3-existence.

Proof.

Given
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
and
M_{ij}
an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{i},M_{j}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, let
N
be an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{3},M_{12}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion. Note that as
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq M_{12}
, by regularity there is
N_{1}\leq N
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

But then by uniqueness, there is a
K
-isomorphism
f_{1}:M_{13}\longrightarrow N_{1}
such that
f_{1}
is the identity on
M_{1}\cup M_{3}
. Defining
f_{2}
analogously via
M_{2}
and
M_{23}
, this proves the statement.
∎

Definition 6.3
.

Given a triple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
, we say that it is a
non-uniqueness triple
if there are models
N_{1},N_{2}
and
K
-embeddings
f_{1},f_{2}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

But there is no
K
-isomorphism
g
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

We say that the tuple
(N_{1},f_{1},N_{2},f_{2})

witnesses
that
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is a non-uniqueness triple.

We say that
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is a
uniqueness triple
if it is not a non-uniqueness triple.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal. If
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is a non-uniqueness triple as witnessed by
(N_{1},f_{1},N_{2},f_{2})
, then for any
N^{\prime}\geq N_{2}
, there is no
K
-embedding
g:N_{1}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Proof.

Let
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2},N_{1},N_{2},f_{1},f_{2},N^{\prime}
be as above, and assume for a contradiction that there does exist a
K
-embedding
g
making the above diagram commute. Note then by Invariance of
\mathscr{A}
,
g[N_{1}]\leq N^{\prime}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1}
and
(g\circ f_{1})[M_{2}]=f_{2}[M_{2}]
over
M_{0}
. Since
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, this implies that
g[N_{1}]=N_{2}
, contradicting that
(N_{1},f_{1},N_{2},f_{2})
is a witness to
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
being a non-uniqueness triple.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous. Let
\delta
be a limit and
(M_{i})_{i\leq\delta},(N_{i})_{i<\delta}
be increasing continuous chains such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

If each
(M_{i},N_{i},M_{i+1})
is a uniqueness triple, then
(M_{0},N_{0},M_{\delta})
is also a uniqueness triple.

Proof.

Let
N^{1},f_{1},N^{2},f_{2}
be such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Inductively, define
N_{i}^{1},g_{i}^{1},N_{i}^{2},g_{i}^{2}
for
i<\delta
such that:

(1)

N_{0}^{1}=f_{1}[N_{0}]\leq N^{1},N_{0}^{2}=f_{2}[N_{0}]\leq N^{2}

(2)

g_{0}^{1}=f_{1},g_{0}^{2}=f_{2}

(3)

For
k=1,2
,
(N_{i}^{k})_{i<\delta}
is a continuous resolution of
N^{k}

(4)

For
k=1,2
and
i<\delta
, the following is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

(5)

Each
g_{i}^{k}:N_{i}\cong N_{i}^{k}
is an isomorphism such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i+1}^{k}}

Note that taking
g^{k}=\bigcup_{i<\delta}g_{i}^{k}
and
h=g^{2}\circ(g^{1})^{-1}
shows that
(N^{1},f_{1},N^{2},f_{2})
is not a witness to non-uniqueness. Proceeding with the induction, note that only the successor step requires verification.

So given
N_{i}^{k},g_{i}^{k}
for
k=1,2
, let
N_{i+1}^{k}:=N_{0}^{k}\oplus_{M_{0}}^{N^{k}}M_{i+1}
. Note then that
N_{i+1}^{k}
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{i}^{k},M_{i+1}
over
M_{i}
by inclusion. Furthermore, as
g_{i}^{k}:N_{i}\cong_{M_{i}}N_{i}^{k}
is an isomorphism and
(M_{i},N_{i},M_{i+1})
is a uniqueness triple, hence
g_{i+1}^{k}
satisfying (5) exists.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular and absolutely minimal. If
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is a non-uniqueness triple and
N\geq M_{2}
, then
(M_{0},M_{1},N)
is also a non-uniqueness triple.

Proof.

We will show the contrapositive and assume that
(M_{0},M_{1},N)
is a uniqueness triple. Let
M_{1}^{*},f_{1},M_{2}^{*},f_{2}
be two
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

Further, let
N^{1},g_{1},N^{2},g_{2}
be
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

By regularity, each
N^{k},g_{k}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{0},M_{1},N)
, and hence by the assumption that this is a uniqueness triple there is an isomorphism
h:N^{1}\cong N^{2}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}

In particular, since
g_{k}
extends
f_{k}
for both
k=1,2
,

h[f_{1}[M_{2}]]=(h\circ g_{1})[M_{2}]=g_{2}[M_{2}]=f_{2}[M_{2}]

Now, as
M_{*}^{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},f_{1}[M_{2}]
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, by invariance
h[M_{*}^{1}]
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},f_{2}[M_{2}]
over
M_{0}
by inclusion. Hence, by absolute minimality,
h[M_{*}^{1}]=M_{*}^{2}
, and in particular
h\upharpoonright M_{*}^{1}
is an isomorphism such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{*}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\upharpoonright M_{*}^{1}}
\cong
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}

In particular, since
M_{*}^{1},M_{*}^{2}
are two arbitrary
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
, hence
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
is also a uniqueness triple.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, regular, and continuous. If there is a non-uniqueness triple
(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
, then for any
\lambda\geq|M_{1}|+|M_{2}|
there is a non-uniqueness triple
(M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
such that

|M_{0}|\leq|M_{0}^{\prime}|=|M_{1}^{\prime}|\leq|M_{1}|\leq|M_{2}^{\prime}|=\lambda

Proof.

Fix
N,f
be a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Fix
(M^{(i)})_{i<|M_{1}|}
be a continuous resolution of
M_{1}
with
M^{(0)}=M_{0}
, and by Lemma
2.13
let
(N^{(i)})_{i<|M_{1}|}
be a continuous resolution of
N
such that each
N^{(i)}
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\upharpoonright M^{(i)}}

By Lemma
6.5
, there is some
i<|M_{1}|
such that
(M^{(i)},M^{(i+1)},N^{(i)})
is a non-uniqueness triple. Letting
M_{0}^{\prime}=M^{(i)},M_{1}^{\prime}=M^{(i+1)}
, and taking
M_{2}^{\prime}\geq N^{(i)}
with
|M_{2}^{\prime}|=\lambda
, then the above lemma shows that
(M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
is a non-uniqueness triple.
∎

Theorem 6.8
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal and has weak 3-existence. If
(M_{b},M^{*},M)
is a non-uniqueness triple and
p=\text{gtp}(M^{*}/M_{b},M^{*})
, then there is
N\geq M
such that
p
has
2^{|N|}
-many extensions to
N
.

Proof.

Since
(M_{b},M^{*},M)
is a non-uniqueness triple, fix
M^{0},M^{1}
two
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams of
M^{*},M
over
M_{b}
by inclusion such that there is no
K
-isomorphism from
M^{0}
to
M^{1}
fixing
M^{*}\cup M
pointwise. Define
\lambda:=|M|+\text{LS}(K)
, and let
N
be such that
N
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
over
M_{b}
by inclusion, with isomorphisms
g_{i}:M_{i}\cong_{M_{b}}M
. In particular, this means that there is a continuous resolution
(N_{i})_{i<\lambda}
such that:

(1)

N_{0}=M_{b}
and
N_{1}=M_{0}

(2)

For each
i<\lambda

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

To prove the theorem, for every
\eta\in{\vphantom{2}}^{\lambda}2
we will construct
M_{\eta}
a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},N
over
M_{b}
, and such that for
\xi\neq\eta
,
\text{gtp}(M^{*}/N,M_{\xi})\neq\text{gtp}(M^{*}/N,M_{\eta})
. So given
\eta\in{\vphantom{2}}^{\lambda}2
, let us construct an increasing continuous chain
(M_{\eta,i})_{i<\lambda}
and embeddings
(h_{\eta,i})_{i<\lambda}
such that:

(1)

M_{\eta,0}=M^{*}
and
h_{\eta,0}=\iota:M_{b}\hookrightarrow M^{*}

(2)

(h_{\eta,i}:N_{i}\longrightarrow M_{\eta,i})_{i<\lambda}
is an increasing sequence

(3)

For each
i<\lambda

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i}}

(4)

For each
i<\lambda

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i+1}}

(5)

For each
i<\lambda
, there is a
K
-embedding
f_{\eta,i}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\eta(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i+1}}

We proceed to construct by induction:

•

For
i=0
, define
M_{\eta,0}=M^{*}
and
h_{\eta,0}=\iota
as specified.

•

For limit
\alpha<\lambda
, let
M_{\eta,\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}M_{\eta,i}
, and similarly
h_{\eta,\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}h_{\eta,\alpha}
. Note that by (4) and continuity, this implies that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,\alpha}}

•

Given
M_{\eta,i}
and
h_{\eta,i}
defined, note that we have
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\eta(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i}}

Hence, as
\mathscr{A}
has weak 3-existence, there exists a model
M_{\eta,i+1}
and maps
f_{\eta,i},h_{\eta,i+1}
such that
M_{\eta,i+1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M^{*},N_{i+1}
over
M_{b}
and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\eta(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

In particular, by regularity the following commutative squares are also
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta,i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta,i+1}}

Letting
M_{\eta}:=\bigcup_{i<\lambda}M_{\eta,i}
and
h_{\eta}=\bigcup_{i<\lambda}h_{\eta,i}
, note then that
h_{\eta}
is a
K
-embedding from
N
to
M_{\eta}
which fixes
M_{b}
pointwise.

To complete the proof, it remains to show that for any
\xi\neq\eta
, there is no
M^{\prime}\geq M_{\eta}
and a
K
-embedding
F
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\xi}[N]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta}[N]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta}\circ h_{\xi}^{-1}}

So suppose for a contradiction that such a
M^{\prime},F
exists. Since
\xi\neq\eta
, fix
i_{0}<\lambda
such that
\xi(i_{0})\neq\eta(i_{0})
. Assuming WLOG that
\eta(i_{0})=0
, by construction of
M_{\eta}
we have that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M_{\eta}}h_{\eta}[M_{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta,i_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\eta}}

Similarly, since
\xi(i_{0})=1
, we have that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M_{\xi}}h_{\xi}[M_{i}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\xi,i_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\xi}}

But note that as
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal and
F
is a
K
-embedding,

F[M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M_{\xi}}h_{\xi}[M_{i_{0}}]]=M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M^{\prime}}h_{\eta}[M_{i_{0}}]=M^{*}\oplus_{M_{b}}^{M\eta}h_{\eta}[M_{i_{0}}]

This contradicts that
(M^{0},M^{1})
is a witness to
(M_{b},M^{*},M)
being a non-uniqueness triple.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

Suppose
\mathscr{A}
is regular, continuous, absolutely minimal, and has weak 3-existence. If
\mathscr{A}
does not have uniqueness, then there is some
M\in K
and a
p\in S^{\text{LS}(K)+|M|}(M)
such that for every
\lambda\geq\text{LS}(K)+|M|
, there is
N\in K_{\lambda}
such that
N\geq M
and
p
has
2^{\lambda}
(nonforking) extensions to
N
.

In particular, if we assume that
K
is sufficiently type-short and
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of geometric amalgamation with weak 3-existence, then
\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness iff
K
is
\lambda
-stable on a tail of cardinals.

7.
Classes with Pregeometries and Regular Types

One last example which we would like to consider is the following: let
T
be the first order theory in a 2-sorted language, such that models of
T
are of the form
(V,F)
, where
F
is a field and
V
is a vector space over
F
. Whilst
T
is clearly not categorical in any cardinal, the uncountable categoricity of vector spaces implies that categoricity transfer holds in the subclass where
F
is fixed. More generally, if we consider the vectors in a model of
T
to (essentially) realize a regular type, and define the class
K
where each model consists of the realization of the fixed regular type within a model in
T
, then
K
also has satisfies some categoricity transfer. In this sense, we wish to prove an analogous result for an AEC with some given notion of independence. This can be seen (essentially) as a case of Zilber’s categoricity result for quasiminimal excellent classes from
[
Zil05
]
(see also
[
KIR10
]
and chapter 2 of
[
Bal09
]
), and more specifically as a quasiminimal AEC introduced by Vasey in
[
Vas18
]
. However, we will be using the categoricity results of section 5 to provide an alternative proof.

Recall that if
T
is a stable first order theory, then the realizations of a regular type within a model form a pregeometry (where independence is forking independence). It is hence helpful for us to first investigate how an AEC where each model is a pregeometry admits a notion of amalgamation:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
K
be an AEC. A
system of pregeometries
for
K
consists of functions
(\text{cl}_{M})_{M\in K}
such that:

(1)

For each
M\in K
,
(M,\text{cl}_{M})
is a pregeometry i.e.
\text{cl}_{M}:\mathscr{P}(M)\longrightarrow\mathscr{P}(M)
satisfies:

(a)

For each
X\subseteq M
,
X\subseteq\text{cl}_{M}(X)=\text{cl}_{M}(\text{cl}_{M}(X))

(b)

If
X\subseteq Y
, then
\text{cl}_{M}(X)\subseteq\text{cl}_{M}(Y)

(c)

If
a\in\text{cl}_{M}(X)
, then there exists
X_{0}\subseteq X
such that
|X|<\aleph_{0}
and
a\in\text{cl}_{M}(X_{0})

(d)

If
b\in\text{cl}_{M}(A\cup\{a\})-\text{cl}_{M}(A)
, then
a\in\text{cl}_{M}(A\cup\{b\})

(2)

If
M\leq N
, then
\text{cl}_{M}\subseteq\text{cl}_{N}
. In particular,
M=\text{cl}_{M}(M)=\text{cl}_{N}(M)

(3)

If
B\subseteq N
,
B=\text{cl}_{N}(B)
, and there exists some
M_{0}\leq N
such that
M_{0}\subseteq\text{cl}_{N}(B)
, then there is some
M\leq N
such that
B
is the universe of
M
.

Given
M\in K
and
B\subseteq M
, we say that
B
is
closed
if
B
is a closed set relative to
\text{cl}_{M}
. We will similarly use terminology for pregeometries (independent sets, etc.) without explicit references to the ambient model.

Remark
.

The assumption that each closure operator is finitary is necessary for
K
to be an AEC: if
\text{cl}_{N}
is not finitary, the union of a chain of closed sets might not be closed, and thus
K
violates the Tarski-Vaught chain axioms. More generally, if each
\text{cl}_{N}
has
<\lambda
-character, then
K
is a
\lambda
-AEC.

Definition 7.2
.

Given
(\text{cl}_{M})_{M\in K}
a system of pregeometries for
K
and AEC, we define
\mathscr{A}
to be a notion of amalgamation on
K
by asserting that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

if and only if there is
B_{1},B_{2}\subseteq N
such that

(1)

B_{1}\cup B_{2}
is an independent set and
\text{cl}_{N}(B_{1}\cup B_{2})=N

(2)

\text{cl}_{N}(B_{1})=M_{1}
and
\text{cl}_{N}(B_{2})=M_{2}

(3)

\text{cl}_{N}(B_{1}\cap B_{2})=M_{0}

Lemma 7.3
.

\mathscr{A}
as defined above is 3-monotonic, absolutely minimal, continuous, and admits decomposition.

Proof.

(1)

3-monotonicity follows straightforwardly from the definition of
\mathscr{A}

(2)

For absolute minimality, suppose
N
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion. Hence there are
B_{1},B_{2}\subseteq N
such that
\text{cl}_{N}(B_{1}\cup B_{2})=N
and
\text{cl}_{N}(B_{i})=M_{i}
, and therefore
\text{cl}_{N}(M_{1}\cup M_{2})=N
. Now, if
N^{\prime}\geq N
and
M^{\prime}\leq N^{\prime}
is such that
M_{1}\cup M_{2}\subseteq M^{\prime}
, then

N=\text{cl}_{N}(M_{1}\cup M_{2})=\text{cl}_{N^{\prime}}(M_{1}\cup M_{2})=\text{cl}_{M^{\prime}}(M_{1}\cup M_{2})\subseteq M^{\prime}

This shows that
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal.

(3)

For continuity, suppose
\delta
is a limit ordinal and there are models
(M_{i},N_{i})_{i<\delta}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Inductively, we will define sets
B,(A_{i})_{i<\delta}
such that:

(a)

B\subseteq N_{0}
and
A_{i}\subseteq M_{i}

(b)

(A_{i})_{i<\delta}
is an increasing continuous sequence of sets

(c)

For each
i<\delta
,
B\cup A_{i}
is independent, and
B\cap A_{i}=A_{0}

(d)

\text{cl}_{N_{0}}(B)=N_{0}

(e)

For each
i<\delta
,
\text{cl}_{M_{i}}(A_{i})=M_{i}

(f)

For each
i<\delta
,
\text{cl}_{N_{i}}(B\cup A_{i})=N_{i}

Note that this is sufficient: letting
A_{\delta}=\bigcup_{i<\delta}
,
A_{\delta}
is a basis for
\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i}
,
B\cup A_{\delta}
is independent, and
B\cap A_{\delta}=A_{0}
. Moreover, since each
N_{i}=\text{cl}_{N_{i}}(B\cup A_{i})
, hence
B\cup A_{\delta}
is a basis for
\bigcup_{i<\delta}N_{i}
. Thus the basis
B\cup A_{\delta}
witnesses that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i<\delta}M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

So let us construct the sets
B,(A_{i})_{i<\delta}
:

•

Since
N_{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},N_{0}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, fix
B,A_{1}
a basis of
N_{0},M_{1}
respectively that witnesses the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam, and let
A_{0}=B\cap A_{1}
.

•

For limit
\alpha
, let
A_{\alpha}=\bigcup_{i<\alpha}A_{i}
as required.

•

Given
A_{i}
, by induction
A_{i}
is a basis for
M_{i}
,
B\cup A_{i}
is a basis for
N_{i}
, and
N_{i+1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{i+1},N_{i}
over
M_{i}
. By the exchange property, thus there is
A_{i+1}
a basis of
M_{i+1}
which extends
A_{i}
and such that
B\cup A_{i+1}
is independent. Moreover, thus
B\cap A_{i+1}\subseteq M_{i}
, and hence by induction
B\cap A_{i+1}=B\cap A_{i}=A_{0}
.

This completes the proof for continuity.

(4)

For decomposability, suppose
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq N
. Fix
A_{0}
a basis of
M_{0}
. and extend to
A_{1}
a basis of
M_{1}
. Extending further to
B
a basis for
N
, let
M_{2}=\text{cl}_{N}(A_{0}\cup(B-A_{1}))
. Then
N
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion, as required.

∎

Lemma 7.4
.

\mathscr{A}
as defined above is regular.

Proof.

Recalling the definition of regularity (Definition
2.1
), we shall prove the implications
2\Rightarrow 1\Rightarrow 3\Rightarrow 2

•

(
2\Rightarrow 1
) Suppose that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Fix independent sets
A_{0}^{1},A_{1}^{1},A_{1}^{2},A_{2}^{2},B_{0}^{1},B_{1}^{1},B_{1}^{2}
such that:

(1)

A_{0}^{1}
is a basis for
M_{0}
,
B_{0}^{1}
is a basis for
N_{0}

(2)

A_{1}^{1},A_{1}^{2}
are bases for
M_{1}
,
B_{1}^{1},B_{1}^{2}
are bases for
N_{1}

(3)

A_{2}^{2}
is a basis for
M_{2}

(4)

A_{0}^{1}=B_{0}^{1}\cap A_{1}^{1}
,
B_{1}^{1}=A_{1}^{1}\cup B_{0}^{1}
, and
A_{1}^{2}=B_{1}^{2}\cap A_{2}^{2}

(5)

A_{2}^{2}\cup B_{1}^{2}
is a basis for
N_{2}

By applying the exchange property, we can find
A_{2}^{1}
which extends
A_{1}^{1}
and is a basis for
M_{2}
. Since
\text{cl}_{N_{1}}(B_{1}^{1})=\text{cl}_{N_{1}}(B_{1}^{2})
and
A_{2}^{2}\cup B_{1}^{2}
is independent with
A_{2}^{2}\cap B_{1}^{2}\subseteq M_{1}
, hence
B_{0}^{1}\cup A_{2}^{1}
is also independent. Hence
N_{2}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{2},N_{0}
over
M_{0}
by inclusion.

•

(
1\Rightarrow 3
) Suppose that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Further, let
M_{1}
be such that
M_{0}\leq M_{1}\leq M_{2}
. Now, as
N_{2}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
N_{0},M_{2}
over
M_{0}
, there is a basis
B
of
N_{2}
such that
B\cap N_{0},B\cap M_{2},B\cap M_{0}
are all bases of the respective models. So extend
B\cap M_{0}
to
B_{1}
, a basis of
M_{1}
, and note that
B_{1}\cup(B\cap N_{0})
is still independent as
M_{1}\leq M_{2}
. So taking
N_{1}=\text{cl}_{N_{2}}(B_{1}\cup(B\cap N_{0}))
, we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Furthermore, we can extend
B_{1}
to a basis
B_{2}
of
M_{2}
, and still maintain that
B_{2}\cup(B_{1}\cup(B\cap N_{0}))=B_{2}\cup(B\cap N_{0})
is independent. Hence
N_{2}
is also an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{2},N_{1}
over
M_{1}
. Note that this is sufficient to show
1\Rightarrow 3
, since
\mathscr{A}
being absolutely minimal implies that
N_{1}
is the unique
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
M_{1},N_{0}
over
M_{0}
inside
N_{2}
.

•

(
3\Rightarrow 2
) This is trivial.

∎

Lemma 7.5
.

For
\mathscr{A}
as defined above,
\mu(K)=\aleph_{0}

Proof.

This is straightforward from the fact that each closure operator has finite character.
∎

Since we are interested in types which have
U
-rank 1, we require the class
K
to admit some suitable notion of nonforking. For this, we use the notions of stable and simple independence given in
[
GM20
]
, which extends earlier work in
[
Bon+16
]
and
[
LRV19
]
. The reader is encouraged to consult
[
GM20
]
for the relevant definition.

Fact 7.6

(
[
GM20
]
, Proposition 5.9)
.

Suppose
N
is a monster model in
K
, and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a simple independence relation on
N
. If
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}B
, then there is a model
M^{\prime}\geq M
such that
B\subseteq M^{\prime}
and
A\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}M^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose
N
is a monster model in
K
,
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a supersimple (in particular, simple) independence relation on
N
with the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property for singletons, and
p\in S^{1}(M_{0})
is a Galois type with
U(p)=1
. Define the operator
\text{cl}_{p}
on
p(N)
by:

\text{cl}_{p}(A):=\{x\in p(N):x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A\}

Then
\text{cl}_{p}
is a closure operator on
p(N)
, and
(p(N),\text{cl}_{p})
is a pregeometry.

Proof.

We first need a claim:

Claim
.

If
M\geq M_{0}
and
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}A
, then for every model
M^{*}\geq M
with
A\subseteq M^{*}
,
x\in M^{*}
also.

Proof.

Otherwise, if
M^{*}\geq M\geq M_{0}
is such that
A\subseteq M^{*}
but
x\notin M^{*}
, note then as
x\in p(N)
,
\text{gtp}(x/M^{*},N)
must be the unique nonalgebraic extension of
p
to
M^{*}
, and hence is the nonforking extension of
p
to
M^{*}
. Thus by transitivity
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}M^{*}
, contradicting
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}A
.
∎

We can now show the properties required of
\text{cl}_{p}
:

•

\text{cl}_{p}
is monotonic: for every
a\in A
,
a\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A

•

\text{cl}_{p}
is idempotent: let
X,y
be such that
y\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A\cup X
and for every
x\in X,x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
. Suppose for a contradiction that
y\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
, so by Fact
7.6
there is some
M\geq M_{0}
such that
A\subseteq M
and
y\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}M
. Note that since each
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
, by the above claim
X\subseteq M
, and hence in particular
y\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A\cup X
, a contradiction.

•

\text{cl}_{p}
has finite character: If
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
, then by the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property there must be a finite
A_{0}\subseteq A
such that
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A_{0}
.

•

\text{cl}_{p}
satisfies the exchange property: Suppose that
x\in\text{cl}_{p}(A\cup\{b\})-\text{cl}_{p}(A)
. Hence
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
, and
b\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
. Now, let
M
be a model such that
M_{0}\leq M
and
A\subseteq M
: note that since
x\in\text{cl}_{p}(A\cup\{b\})
, by the above claim, for every model
M^{\prime}\geq M_{0}
, if
b\in M^{\prime}
then
x\in M^{\prime}
. In particular, this holds for any
M^{\prime}\geq M
. But then by the previous facts this implies that
x\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}b
, and hence by symmetry
b\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}x
for any such arbitrary
M
. So assume for a contradiction that
b\notin\text{cl}_{p}(A\cup\{x\})
, and hence there must be some model
M\geq M_{0}
such that
A\cup\{x\}\subseteq M
but
b\notin M
. This contradicts that
b\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M}x
.

∎

Remark
.

The assumption that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
has the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property may appear at first to be very strong, but it was shown in
[
GM20
]
(Theorem 7.12 and Corollary 7.16) that having bounded
U
-rank is equivalent to
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
being supersimple, which for classes with (arbitrary) intersection implies that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
does have the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property. Since the assumption of
U(p)=1
is necessary for the construction in consideration here, assuming that
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
does have the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property does not significantly increase the strength of our assumptions in totality.

Definition 7.8
.

Suppose
K
is an AEC in a relational language
\tau
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is a supersimple independence relation with the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property for singletons on a monster model
N
of
K
. Let
p\in S^{1}(M_{0})
be a Galois type such that
U(p)=1
. We define the abstract class
(K_{p},\leq_{p})
, where:

(1)

\tau(K_{p})=\tau_{M_{0}}=\tau\sqcup\{c_{a}\}_{a\in M_{0}}
, where each
c_{a}
is a new constant symbol.

(2)

A
\tau_{M_{0}}
structure
\mathscr{M}
is a model in
K_{p}
iff there is a
\tau_{M_{0}}
-embedding
f
from
\mathscr{M}
into a set
A\cup M_{0}\subseteq N
, such that:

•

A\subseteq p(N)
and
A
is closed with respect to
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}
i.e. if
b\in p(N)
and
b\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}A
, then
b\in A
.

•

f(c_{a}^{\mathscr{M}})=a

(3)

\mathscr{M}_{1}\leq_{p}\mathscr{M}_{2}
iff there is a
\tau_{M_{0}}
-isomorphism
f:\mathscr{M}\longrightarrow p(N)\cup M_{0}
such that both
f
and
f\upharpoonright\mathscr{M}_{1}
satisfies the above conditions.

Remark
.

Of course,
K_{p}
as defined above is not strictly an AEC since all of its models are of bounded cardinality. However, by the lemma below, given some monster model
N^{\prime}>N
with a corresponding notion of independence, we can use
N^{\prime}
to extend
K_{p}
, and so in particular
K_{p}
as already defined contains all ”small” models.

Lemma 7.9
.

K_{p}
is an AEC with a system of pregeometries inherited from
N
,
\text{LS}(K_{p})=|M_{0}|+\text{LS}(K)
, and
M_{0}
as a
\tau_{M_{0}}
structure is prime and minimal in
K
.

Proof.

Having fixed
N
a monster model of
K
and
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
a stable independence relation on
N
, let us first describe the system of pregeometries: for any
M\in K_{p}
,
M=(A,M_{0})
where there is a
\tau
-embedding
f
such that
f[A]\subseteq p(N)
,
f\upharpoonright M_{0}=\text{id}_{M_{0}}
, and
f[A]
is closed w.r.t.
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
. We define
\text{cl}_{M}
by:

(1)

\text{cl}_{M}(\varnothing)=\text{cl}_{M}(M_{0})=M_{0}

(2)

For any
B
,
\text{cl}_{M}(B)=\text{cl}_{M}(B\cup M_{0})

(3)

For
B\subseteq A
,
\text{cl}_{M}(B)=M_{0}\cup\{x\in A:f(x)\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil{\sl/\/}\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule height=10.0pt,depth=-2.0pt,width=0.3pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits_{M_{0}}f[B]\}

Note that as
f
is a
\tau
-isomorphism from
A
to
f[A]
,
\text{cl}_{M}
as defined above is independent of the choice of
f
as
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
is invariant under
\tau
-automorphisms of
N
. The other conditions for the closure operators to be a system of pregeometries for
K_{p}
follows straightforwardly. Moreover, since any
\tau_{M_{0}}
-embedding must be the identity on
M_{0}
,
M_{0}
is indeed prime and minimal in
K_{p}
.
∎

Definition 7.10
.

Given
(X,\text{cl})
a pregeometry and closed sets
A_{0},A_{1},A_{2}\subseteq X
, we say that
A_{1},A_{2}

are independent over

A_{0}
if there are independent sets
B_{1},B_{2}
such that:

•

\text{cl}(B_{1})=A_{1},\text{cl}(B_{2})=A_{2}

•

\text{cl}(B_{1}\cap B_{2})=A_{0}

•

B_{1}\cup B_{2}
is an independent set.

We say that the pair
(B_{1},B_{2})
is a
witness
to
A_{1},A_{2}
being independent over
A_{0}
. Note that if
A_{1},A_{2}
are independent over
A_{0}
, then
A_{1}\cap A_{2}=A_{0}
.

Theorem 7.11
.

Given
K_{p}
as defined above, if
\mathscr{A}
is defined using the system of pregeometries inherited from
N
, then it has uniqueness.

Proof.

Since the system of pregeometries of
K_{p}
are inherited from the pregeometry
(N,\text{cl}_{p})
and
\mathscr{A}
is defined by independence w.r.t the system of pregeometries, it suffices to prove that:

Claim
.

Suppose
A_{1},A_{2}
are closed subsets of
p(N)
and independent over
A_{0}
. If
f,g
are
\tau_{M_{0}}
-automorphisms of
N
such that
f\upharpoonright A_{0}=g\upharpoonright A_{0}
and
f[A_{1}],g[A_{2}]
are independent over
f[A_{0}]
, then there is
h
a
\tau_{M_{0}}
-automorphism of
N
which is an isomorphism between
\text{cl}(A_{1}\cup A_{2})
and
\text{cl}(f[A_{1}]\cup g[A_{2}])
.

So to prove the claim, fix
(B_{1},B_{2})
which witnesses that
A_{1},A_{2}
are independent over
A_{0}
, and let
B_{0}:=B_{1}\cap B_{2}
. Letting
\lambda=|B_{2}-B_{0}|
, fix also an enumeration
B_{2}-B_{0}=\{b_{i}:i<\lambda\}
, and we will construct a sequence
(h_{i}:i<\lambda)
such that:

(1)

Each
h_{i}
is a restriction of a
\tau_{M_{0}}
-automorphism of
N
, and the sequence is an increasing continuous chain

(2)

h_{0}=f\upharpoonright A_{1}

(3)

For each
i<\lambda
,
\text{dom }h_{i}=\text{cl}_{p}(A_{1}\cup\{b_{j}:j<\lambda\})=:A_{1}^{i}

(4)

For each
i<\lambda
,
h_{i}\upharpoonright\text{cl}_{p}(b_{j}:j<i)=g\upharpoonright\text{cl}_{p}(b_{j}:j<i)

This is sufficient, as letting
h=\bigcup_{i<\lambda}h_{i}
gives the desired automorphism. So let us proceed inductively:

•

For
i=0
, take
h_{0}=f\upharpoonright A_{1}
as required.

•

At limit stages, we take the union as required.

•

If
h_{i}
is constructed with
h_{i}=h^{*}\upharpoonright A_{1}^{i}
and
A_{1}^{i}=\text{cl}_{p}(A_{1}\cup\{b_{j}:j<i\})
for some
h^{*}
a
\tau_{M_{0}}
-automorphism of
N
, note that as
B_{2}
is independent by assumption,
b_{i}
is independent from
A_{1}^{i}
, and so is
h^{*}(b_{i})
from
h_{i}[A_{1}^{i}]
. Hence there is some model
M_{1}
such that
h_{i}[A_{1}^{i}]\subseteq M_{1}
but
h^{*}(b_{i})\notin M_{1}
. Similarly,
g(b_{i})
is independent from
f[A_{1}]\cup g[\text{cl}_{p}(b_{j}:j<i)]=h_{i}[A_{1}^{i}]
, and we can find a model
M_{2}
similarly with
g(b_{i})\notin M_{2}
. Now, let
y\in p(N)
be such that
y\notin M_{1},M_{2}
: in particular,
y
is independent from
M_{1}
over
M_{0}
, and as
U(p)=1
thus
\text{gtp}(y/M_{1},N)=\text{gtp}(h^{*}(b_{i})/M_{1},N)
. Similarly,
\text{gtp}(y/M_{2},N)=\text{gtp}(y/M_{2},N)
. Note that since
A_{1}^{i}\subseteq M_{1}\cap M_{2}
by construction, this implies that there is some automorphism
h^{\prime}
of
N
such that:

–

h^{\prime}\upharpoonright A_{1}^{i}=h_{i}
: and

–

(h^{\prime}\circ h^{*})(b_{i})=g(b_{i})

So we can take
h_{i+1}=h^{\prime}\upharpoonright\text{cl}_{p}(A_{1}^{i}\cup\{b_{i}\})
(possibly by composing with a suitable automorphism of
N
to ensure
h_{i+1}\upharpoonright\text{cl}_{p}(b_{j}:j<i+1)=g\upharpoonright\text{cl}_{p}(b_{j}:j<i+1)

This completes the construction, and hence the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

For any
\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2}\in K_{p}
with
|\mathscr{M}_{1}|=|\mathscr{M}_{2}|=|M_{0}|
,
(\mathscr{M}_{1},M_{0})\backsim(\mathscr{M}_{2},M_{0})

Proof.

Note that if
|\mathscr{M}|=|M_{0}|
, then
\mathscr{M}^{\theta(K_{p})}/M_{0}=\mathscr{M}^{|M_{0}|}/M_{0}=(A,M_{0})
where
A
has dimension
|M_{0}|
as a pregeometry. Since
U(p)=1
, if
b_{1},b_{2}
are both independent from
A
, then there is some
\tau_{M_{0}}
-automorphism of
N
which fixes
A
pointwise but sends
b_{1}
to
b_{2}
. This provides the desired
\tau_{M_{0}}
-isomorphism between
\mathscr{M}_{1}^{\theta(K_{p})}/M_{0}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}^{\theta(K_{p})}/M_{0}
.
∎

Theorem 7.13
.

Suppose
K
has a monster model and a supersimple independence relation with the
(<\aleph_{0})
-witness property for singletons. If
U(p)=1
, then
K_{p}
is
\lambda
-categorical in all
\lambda>|\text{dom }p|+\+LS(K)

Proof.

We have shown that
\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation for
K_{p}
, and that
M_{0}
is a prime and minimal model for
K_{p}
. Furthermore, the above lemma establishes that for there is a unique
\backsim
class for models of cardinality
|M_{0}|=\text{LS}(K_{p})
, so the proof of Theorem
5.6
also applies here. Furthermore, as stated in Theorem
5.6
, we can improve the cardinality transfer bound to
\text{LS}(K_{p})+I(K_{p},\text{LS}(K_{p}))
; but the above lemma establishes that
I(K_{p},\text{LS}(K_{p}))=\text{LS}(K_{p})
, which gives the desired bound.
∎

Corollary 7.14
.

For any
M_{1},M_{2}\in K
, if
M_{0}\leq M_{1},M_{2}
and
|p(M_{1})|=|p(M_{2})|>|M_{0}|
, then
p(M_{1})\cong_{M_{0}}p(M_{2})
as
\tau
-structures.

8.
Open questions

There are some questions that arise immediately from our treatment of notions of amalgamation but which we have yet to answer. For example:

Question 8.1
.

How do we define an independence relation
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
from
\mathscr{A}
(as was done in section 4) but without the assumption of finite intersections?

Note that the categoricity result of section 5 does not assume that the class admits finite intersections, and the canonicity of forking established in
[
Bon+16
]
implies that there should be a canonical notion of forking which is equivalent to
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
as defined in section 4 if the class does have FI. This suggest that there should be a “correct” definition of
\mathop{\hbox{\lower 2.0pt\hbox to12.77773pt{\hfil\vrule width=0.3pt,depth=-2.0pt,height=10.0pt\hfil}\lower 2.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle\smile$}}}\limits
using only properties of
\mathscr{A}
. Alternatively, it may be the case that having FI follows from having free amalgamation, although we are unaware of any evidence that this should be the case.

Question 8.2
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal, continuous, and regular. Is
\mathscr{A}
having uniqueness equivalent to
K
being Galois stable in some
\lambda
?

Both directions require some further work beyond what we have presented here. In the forward direction, note that despite having defined a well-behaving notion of forking in section 4, we assumed that
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition and in particular this is necessary to establish
\mu(K)
as the cardinal for local character. A more satisfying method would be to show some form of local character without assuming that
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition, and in particular without reference to
\mu(K)
.

In the reverse direction, note that Theorem
6.8
assumes weak 3-existence. On the other hand, there are simple first order theories for which 3-amalgamation of
types
is not possible; it therefore seems plausible that (assuming tameness and shortness) stability implies that there are no non-uniqueness triples.

Question 8.3
.

How can we weaken the property of admitting decomposition? Is admitting decomposition equivalent to being superstable?

A relevant result here is Theorem 4.26 by Mazari-Armida from
[
Maz21
]
, which states (in particular) that for a class
K
of
R
-modules closed under direct sums,
K
(with the pure-submodule ordering) is superstable iff every module in
K
is pure-injective. Defining
\mathscr{A}
to be amalgamation by direct sums, we note that every module of
K
being pure-injective implies that
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition. Furthermore, it is straightforward to check that for any
K
and a notion
\mathscr{A}
of free amalgamation on
K
, that
\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition implies that
K
is superstable (via uniqueness of limit models). On the other hand, the result of
[
Maz21
]
depends heavily on a corresponding Galois types with syntactic types, and hence it seems likely that any development in this direction would require at least tameness and shortness.

Question 8.4
.

If
K
is eventually categorical, must
K
admit a notion of free amalgamation? If
K
has uniqueness of limit models, does the subclass of limit models (with the “limit over” ordering) admit a notion of free amalgamation?

This is of course true when
K
is the elementary class of a countable first order theory based on the Baldwin-Lachlan argument for Morley’s categoricity theorem. Hyttinen and Kangas also showed in
[
HK18
]
that universal classes which are eventually categorical are essentially either vector spaces or disintegrated, and in either case would admit a notion of free amalgamation. On the other hand, both arguments require two essential steps: finding some “minimal” type on which a pregeometry can be defined, and showing that every model is prime (in
K
) over their realizations of the minimal type; in particular, for a general AEC
K
it is not yet clear to us whether or not there exists such a correspondence between models
M\in K
and the (pre)geometric portions of such models. A simpler question would be whether class of limit models admit free amalgamation under the assumption of uniqueness of limit models; this can be achieved if a universal resolution of
N
over
M
can be “copied” to any
M^{\prime}
a limit model over
M
; essentially reducing back to Shelah’s construction of
(\lambda,2)
-good sets in
[
She83
]
.

Appendix A
The class of free groups as a weak AEC

For this appendix, let
K
be the class of free groups with the ordering
G\leq_{f}H
iff
G
is a free factor of
H
. We will show in detail that
(K,\leq_{f})
is a weak AEC which admits finite intersection and has a notion of free amalgamation; this follows entirely from Perin’s work in
[
Per11
]
, which builds off a series of work by Sela, in particular
[
Sel06
]
and
[
Sel06a
]
.

Notation A.1
.

For any set
X
, we let
F(X)
denote the free group with
X
as the set of generators. For any ordinal
\alpha
, we let
F_{\alpha}
denote the free group with
\alpha
(as a set of ordinals) as the set of generators, so that if
\beta<\alpha
, then
F_{\beta}
is a subgroup of
F_{\alpha}
.

We use
\preccurlyeq
to indicate the relation of being an elementary submodel.

Fact A.2

(
[
Per11
]
, Theorem 1.3)
.

Let
H
be a proper subgroup of
F_{n}
, the free group on
n
-generators. Then
H
is an elementary submodel of
F_{n}
iff
H
is a free factor of
F_{n}
.

In particular, if
X=\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}\}
,
Y\subseteq X
, then
F(Y)\preccurlyeq F(X)
. Note that the result as stated only applies when
X
is finite; however, it is straightforward to see that this implies the same result for free groups of infinite rank:

Lemma A.3
.

For any ordinal
\alpha
,
F_{\alpha}\preccurlyeq F_{\alpha+1}

Proof.

By induction on
\alpha
:

•

When
\alpha
is finite, this follows from Fact
A.2
.

•

Suppose the statement holds for
\alpha
, and for
\beta\leq\alpha
let
G_{\beta}:=F(\beta\cup\{\alpha+1\})
. By induction, we have that each
F_{\beta}\preccurlyeq G_{\beta}
, and hence

F_{\alpha+1}=\bigcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}F_{\beta}\preccurlyeq\bigcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}G_{\beta}=F(\alpha\cup\{\alpha+1\})=F_{\alpha+2}

∎

Corollary A.4
.

For ordinals
\alpha<\beta
,
F_{\alpha}\preccurlyeq F_{\beta}

Corollary A.5
.

For any sets
X\subseteq Y
,
F(X)\preccurlyeq F(Y)

Fact A.6

(Corollary to Kurosh’s Subgroup Theorem)
.

If
F,G
are free factors of
H
, then
F\cap G
is a free factor of both
F
and
G
. In particular, if
F\subseteq G
, then
F
is a free factor of
G
.

Corollary A.7
.

K
admits finite intersection.

Lemma A.8
.

The class
(K,\leq_{f})
is a weak AEC.

Proof.

The only property which is not immediate is Coherence. So suppose that
F\leq_{f}H
,
G\leq_{f}H
, and
F\subseteq G
. Hence
F,G
are both free factors of
H
, and so
F\leq_{f}G
by Fact
A.6
.
∎

Remark
.

It should be noted that
(K,\leq_{f})
is
not
an AEC as it does not satisfy Smoothness, as exemplified by this example from
[
BCS77
]
: Let
X=\{x_{i}:i<\omega\}
, and define
y_{i}:=x_{i}x_{i+1}^{2}
,
G_{i}:=\langle y_{j}:j<i\rangle
. Note then that each
G_{i}
is a free factor of
F(X)
, but
\bigcup_{i<\omega}G_{i}=\langle x_{i}x_{i+1}^{2}\rangle
is not a free factor of
F(X)
.

In
(K,\leq_{f})
, we define the notion of amalgamation
\mathscr{A}
to be the group (nonabelian) free amalgamation: the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam iff there is a set
Y
with subsets
X_{1},X_{2}\leq Y
such that
H=F(Y)
,
G_{1}=F(X_{1})
,
G_{2}=F(X_{2})
, and
G_{0}=F(X_{1}\cap X_{2})
. Equivalently, there exists
G^{\prime}_{1},G^{\prime}_{2}
such that
G_{1}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{1}
,
G_{2}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}
, and
H=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{1}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}
.

Lemma A.9
.

\mathscr{A}
is absolutely minimal.

Proof.

If
H
is a
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},G_{2}
by inclusion over
G_{0}
, then
H=\langle G_{1}\cup G_{2}\rangle
, which is the minimal subgroup containing
G_{1},G_{2}
in
H
(and every extension of
H
).
∎

Lemma A.10
.

If
H_{1}
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},H_{0}
over
G_{0}
by inclusion, and
X_{0},X_{1}
are free bases of
G_{0},G_{1}
respectively such that
X_{0}\leq X_{1}
, then there is a set
Y_{1}\supseteq X_{0}
such that
Y_{1},Y_{1}\cup X_{1}
are free bases of
H_{0},H_{1}
respectively.

Proof.

Translating to free products of groups, the assumption implies that there are groups
G^{\prime},H^{\prime}
such that:

•

G_{1}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}

•

H_{0}=G_{0}\ast H^{\prime}

•

H_{1}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}\ast H^{\prime}

Hence, if
Y^{\prime}
is any free basis of
H^{\prime}
, then letting
Y_{1}=X_{0}\cup Y^{\prime}
gives the desired result.
∎

Lemma A.11
.

\mathscr{A}
is continuous.

Proof.

Given the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Fix a free basis
X_{0}
of
G_{0}
, and let
Y
be such that
X_{0}\cup Y
is a basis for
H_{0}
. By the above lemma, we can find
X_{1}\supseteq X_{0}
such that
X_{1},X_{1}\cup Y
are bases for
G_{1},H_{1}
respectively. Proceeding by induction, we get that
Y\cup\bigcup_{i}X_{i}
is a basis for
\bigcup_{i}H_{i}
, and hence this is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
H_{0},\bigcup_{i}G_{i}
over
G_{0}
by inclusion.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

\mathscr{A}
is regular.

Proof.

Recall the definition of regularity in Definition
2.1
; We will prove that the three statements are equivalent for
\mathscr{A}
.

•

1\Rightarrow 3
: If
H
is an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},G_{2}
over
G_{0}
by inclusion, then
H=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{1}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}
. Now, if
G_{*}
is such that
G_{0}\leq_{f}G^{*}\leq_{f}G_{1}
, then there is some
G^{\prime}_{*}\leq_{f}G^{\prime}_{1}
such that
G_{*}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{*}
. Thus we have that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}\ast G^{\prime}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

•

2\Rightarrow 1
: Assume that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Hence we have that
H_{1}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{1}\ast H^{\prime}
and
H_{2}=G_{1}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}\ast H^{\prime}=G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}_{1}\ast G^{\prime}_{2}\ast H
. So
H_{2}
is indeed an
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{2},H_{0}
over
G_{0}
by inclusion.

•

2\Rightarrow 3
: This is straightforward.

∎

Lemma A.13
.

\mathscr{A}
admits decomposition, and
\mu(K)=\aleph_{0}

Proof.

If
G_{0}\leq_{f}G_{1}\leq_{f}G_{2}
, then there is some
G^{\prime}
such that
G_{2}=G_{1}\ast G^{\prime}
, and hence
G_{2}
is the
\mathscr{A}
-amalgam of
G_{1},G_{0}\ast G^{\prime}
over
G_{0}
by inclusion. That
\mu(K)=\aleph_{0}
is equivalent to the fact that all words in a free group are of finite length.
∎

Lemma A.14
.

\mathscr{A}
has uniqueness.

Proof.

This is straightforward from the fact that free amalgamation is a pushout in the category of groups.
∎

Corollary A.15
.

\mathscr{A}
is a notion of free amalgamation on
(K,\leq_{f})
